    "en": "Mintuci is a water spirit in Ainu mythology and folklore.",
    "claim_ids": [
      "mintuci-c01"
    ]
  },
  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Mintuci adalah roh air dalam mitologi dan cerita rakyat Ainu. Ia digambarkan sebagai makhluk setengah manusia dan setengah hewan. Sebagian kisah menghubungkannya dengan kappa.",
      "en": "Mintuci is a water spirit in Ainu mythology and folklore. It is described as partly human and partly animal. Some accounts relate it to the kappa.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "mintuci-c01",
        "mintuci-c02",
        "mintuci-c03"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Kajian cerita rakyat berbahasa Jepang juga memakai ejaan mintsuchi. Kamus awal menempatkan makhluk ini di danau dan sungai. Satu penjelasan menghubungkan namanya dengan istilah Jepang mizuchi.",
      "en": "Japanese folklore studies also use the spelling mintsuchi. An early dictionary locates the creature in lakes and rivers. One explanation links its name with the Japanese term mizuchi.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "mintuci-c04",
        "mintuci-c05",
        "mintuci-c06"
      ]
    }
  ],
  "cultural_context": null,

  ],
  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "mintuci-c01",
      "source_id": "mintuci-s1",
      "quote": "The mintuci (Ainu ミントゥチ; also mintuci kamuy, also transliterated into Japanese as mintsuchi (ミンツチ)) is a water sprite or an aquatic supernatural creature, a half-man-half-beast, told in stories of Ainu mythology and folklore.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Mintuci adalah roh air dalam mitologi dan cerita rakyat Ainu.",
        "en": "Mintuci is a water spirit in Ainu mythology and folklore."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "mintuci-c02",
      "source_id": "mintuci-s1",
      "quote": "The mintuci (Ainu ミントゥチ; also mintuci kamuy, also transliterated into Japanese as mintsuchi (ミンツチ)) is a water sprite or an aquatic supernatural creature, a half-man-half-beast, told in stories of Ainu mythology and folklore.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ia digambarkan sebagai makhluk setengah manusia dan setengah hewan.",
        "en": "It is described as partly human and partly animal."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "mintuci-c03",
      "source_id": "mintuci-s1",
      "quote": "It is also considered a variant of the kappa and, therefore, a type of yōkai.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Sebagian kisah menghubungkannya dengan kappa.",
        "en": "Some accounts relate it to the kappa."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "mintuci-c04",
      "source_id": "mintuci-s1",
      "quote": "The name is mintuci (Ainu: ミントゥチ) according to modern Ainu orthography, but it is also commonly spelled mintsuchi (ミンツチ) in folkloric study literature written in Japanese.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kajian cerita rakyat berbahasa Jepang juga memakai ejaan mintsuchi.",
        "en": "Japanese folklore studies also use the spelling mintsuchi."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "mintuci-c05",
      "source_id": "mintuci-s1",
      "quote": "The mintuci has been defined as \"fabulous animal\", purported to be \"half human and half animal and to inhabit lakes and rivers\" in the Ainu dictionary c. 1900 compiled by British missionary John Batchelor.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kamus awal menempatkan makhluk ini di danau dan sungai.",
        "en": "An early dictionary locates the creature in lakes and rivers."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "mintuci-c06",
      "source_id": "mintuci-s1",
      "quote": "The Ainu word mintuci is considered to be borrowed from the Japanese word mizuchi (or variants thereof), that are local appellations for the kappa, ultimately deriving from the term mizuchi, which signifies a type of dragon.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "etymology",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Satu penjelasan menghubungkan namanya dengan istilah Jepang mizuchi.",
        "en": "One explanation links its name with the Japanese term mizuchi."
      }
    }
  ],

    "claim_ids": [

    ]
  },
  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Mishaguji adalah sebutan kelompok kami yang dihormati di wilayah Suwa. Mereka berperan penting dalam upacara kuil pada musim dingin dan musim semi.",
      "en": "Mishaguji names a group of kami venerated in the Suwa region. They were important in winter and spring shrine ceremonies.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "mishaguji-c01",
        "mishaguji-c02"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Seorang pemimpin ritual konon memanggil mereka ke dalam manusia atau benda. Ritual itu diakhiri dengan pelepasan roh secara resmi.",
      "en": "A ritual priest was said to summon them into people or objects. The rites concluded with a formal dismissal of the spirits.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "mishaguji-c03",
        "mishaguji-c04"
      ]
    }
  ],
  "cultural_context": null,

      "title": "Mishaguji",
      "publisher": "Wikipedia",

      "author": null
    }
  ],
  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "mishaguji-c01",
      "source_id": "mishaguji-s1",
      "quote": "Mishaguji (御左口神, 御社宮司, 御射宮司, 御社宮神; katakana: ミシャグジ), also known as Misakuji(n), Mis(h)aguchi or Mishakuji among other variants (see below), is a collective term for deities or spirits (kami) venerated in the Suwa region of Nagano Prefecture, particularly associated with the ritual practices of the Upper Shrine (Kamisha) of Suwa Taisha.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Mishaguji adalah sebutan kelompok kami yang dihormati di wilayah Suwa.",
        "en": "Mishaguji names a group of kami venerated in the Suwa region."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "mishaguji-c02",
      "source_id": "mishaguji-s1",
      "quote": "These spirits once played a key role in the shrine’s winter and spring religious ceremonies and are also enshrined in numerous smaller shrines throughout the region.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Mereka berperan penting dalam upacara kuil pada musim dingin dan musim semi.",
        "en": "They were important in winter and spring shrine ceremonies."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "mishaguji-c03",
      "source_id": "mishaguji-s1",
      "quote": "In these ceremonies, the Kan-no-Osa or Jinchō (神長) — also known as Jinchōkan (神長官) — a high-ranking ritual priest from the Moriya clan, would summon the Mishaguji into vessels (yorishiro), such as individuals or objects, to be temporarily inhabited during the rite.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Seorang pemimpin ritual konon memanggil mereka ke dalam manusia atau benda.",
        "en": "A ritual priest was said to summon them into people or objects."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "mishaguji-c04",
      "source_id": "mishaguji-s1",

      "source_id": "moreya-s1",
      "quote": "Moreya or Moriya (洩矢神, Moriya- / Moreya-no-Kami) is a Japanese god who appears in various myths and legends of the Suwa region in Nagano Prefecture (historical Shinano Province).",
      "locator": "Article text",

        "id": "Moreya adalah dewa Jepang dalam legenda wilayah Suwa.",

      }

    {
      "id": "moreya-c02",
      "source_id": "moreya-s1",
      "quote": "The most famous of such stories is that of his battle against Takeminakata, the god of the Grand Shrine of Suwa (Suwa Taisha).",

      "source_id": "moreya-s1",
      "quote": "Moriya is regarded as the mythical ancestor of the Moriya clan (守矢氏), a priestly family that formerly served in the Upper Suwa Shrine (上社, Kamisha), one of the two sub-shrines that make up Suwa Taisha.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Keluarga pendeta Moriya menganggapnya sebagai leluhur mitologis.",

      "id": "moreya-c04",
      "source_id": "moreya-s1",
      "quote": "In addition, he is venerated as a local tutelary deity (ubusunagami) in a shrine in Okaya City near the Tenryū River, which in later variants of the aforementioned myth is identified as the place where Takeminakata and Moriya fought each other.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",

    "claim_ids": [

    ]
  },
  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Nozuchi adalah yōkai Jepang yang kadang ditafsirkan sebagai roh padang. Tubuhnya yang tebal dan mirip ular disamakan dengan palu tanpa gagang. Kisah-kisah memberinya mulut, tetapi tanpa mata atau hidung.",
      "en": "Nozuchi is a Japanese yōkai sometimes interpreted as a field spirit. Its thick, serpentine body is compared with a mallet without a handle. Accounts give it a mouth but no eyes or nose.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "nozuchi-c01",
        "nozuchi-c02",
        "nozuchi-c03"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Ia konon berguling menuruni lereng lalu merayap naik kembali. Satu gambaran memberinya rambut kasar seperti penutup tubuh ulat. Satu kisah daerah di Nara menggambarkannya memakan rusa.",
      "en": "It is said to roll down slopes and slowly climb back. A description gives it bristly hair resembling a caterpillar’s covering. One regional account in Nara describes it eating deer.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "nozuchi-c04",
        "nozuchi-c05",
        "nozuchi-c06"
      ]
    }
  ],
  "cultural_context": null,
  "etymology": null,

  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "nozuchi-c01",
      "source_id": "nozuchi-s1",
      "quote": "野槌（のづち）は、日本に伝わる妖怪。野つ霊、野椎とも。野の精霊（「野つ霊（ち）」の意）であるとも言われる。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Nozuchi adalah yōkai Jepang yang kadang ditafsirkan sebagai roh padang.",
        "en": "Nozuchi is a Japanese yōkai sometimes interpreted as a field spirit."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "nozuchi-c02",
      "source_id": "nozuchi-s1",
      "quote": "外見は蛇のようだが、胴は太く、頭部に口がある以外は目も鼻もなく、ちょうど柄のない槌（つち）のような形をしている。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Tubuhnya yang tebal dan mirip ular disamakan dengan palu tanpa gagang.",
        "en": "Its thick, serpentine body is compared with a mallet without a handle."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "nozuchi-c03",
      "source_id": "nozuchi-s1",
      "quote": "外見は蛇のようだが、胴は太く、頭部に口がある以外は目も鼻もなく、ちょうど柄のない槌（つち）のような形をしている。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kisah-kisah memberinya mulut, tetapi tanpa mata atau hidung.",
        "en": "Accounts give it a mouth but no eyes or nose."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "nozuchi-c04",
      "source_id": "nozuchi-s2",
      "quote": "They are slow movers, and get about by rolling and tumbling down slopes, then slowly inching their way back up.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ia konon berguling menuruni lereng lalu merayap naik kembali.",
        "en": "It is said to roll down slopes and slowly climb back."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "nozuchi-c05",
      "source_id": "nozuchi-s2",
      "quote": "Their bodies are covered in a bristly fur, much like a hairy caterpillar.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Satu gambaran memberinya rambut kasar seperti penutup tubuh ulat.",
        "en": "A description gives it bristly hair resembling a caterpillar’s covering."
      }

    {
      "id": "nozuchi-c06",
      "source_id": "nozuchi-s2",
      "quote": "In Nara, they are known to feed on deer.",

      "context": "traditional-belief",

      "id": "Nuribotoke adalah yōkai yang digambar dalam gulungan makhluk gaib Jepang. Toriyama Sekien juga menggambarnya dalam kumpulan ilustrasi. Gambarnya menyerupai mayat hidup berkulit gelap.",

        "nuribotoke-c02",
        "nuribotoke-c03"
      ]
    },
    {

      "id": "Oboroguruma adalah yōkai kereta dalam mitologi Jepang. Wujudnya kereta lembu gaib yang memiliki wajah. Satu legenda menempatkan bunyi deritnya pada malam berkabut dan diterangi bulan di Kyoto.",
      "en": "Oboroguruma is a cart yōkai in Japanese mythology. Its form is a ghostly ox cart with a face. A legend places its creaking sounds on misty moonlit nights in Kyoto.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "oboroguruma-c01",
        "oboroguruma-c02",
        "oboroguruma-c03"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Kereta itu konon berada di luar rumah seseorang. Toriyama Sekien memuat sosok itu beserta keterangan dalam buku bergambarnya. Satu tafsir menghubungkannya dengan persaingan kereta dalam The Tale of Genji.",
      "en": "The cart is said to wait outside a person’s house. Toriyama Sekien included the figure with a description in his illustrated books. One interpretation links it with the carriage rivalry in The Tale of Genji.",

  ],
  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "oboroguruma-c01",
      "source_id": "oboroguruma-s1",
      "quote": "Oboroguruma (朧車, hazy cart) is a Yōkai in Japanese mythology.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Oboroguruma adalah yōkai kereta dalam mitologi Jepang.",
        "en": "Oboroguruma is a cart yōkai in Japanese mythology."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "oboroguruma-c02",
      "source_id": "oboroguruma-s1",
      "quote": "The Oboroguruma is a ghostly ox cart with a face on it.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Wujudnya kereta lembu gaib yang memiliki wajah.",
        "en": "Its form is a ghostly ox cart with a face."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "oboroguruma-c03",
      "source_id": "oboroguruma-s1",
      "quote": "Legend has it that on misty nights that are lit by the moon in Kyoto, people can hear squeaking sounds coming from an ox cart.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Satu legenda menempatkan bunyi deritnya pada malam berkabut dan diterangi bulan di Kyoto.",
        "en": "A legend places its creaking sounds on misty moonlit nights in Kyoto."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "oboroguruma-c04",
      "source_id": "oboroguruma-s1",
      "quote": "When the person steps outside, they would find the Oboroguruma parked outside their home.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kereta itu konon berada di luar rumah seseorang.",
        "en": "The cart is said to wait outside a person’s house."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "oboroguruma-c05",
      "source_id": "oboroguruma-s2",
      "quote": "When Toriyama Sekien published his yōkai bestiaries, he included the oboroguruma and gave a description.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Toriyama Sekien memuat sosok itu beserta keterangan dalam buku bergambarnya.",
        "en": "Toriyama Sekien included the figure with a description in his illustrated books."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "oboroguruma-c06",
      "source_id": "oboroguruma-s2",
      "quote": "He linked it to a famous scene in The Tale of Genji when Lady Rokujō and her rival Lady Aoi competed for a parking space and got into a carriage fight.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "scholarly-interpretation",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Satu tafsir menghubungkannya dengan persaingan kereta dalam The Tale of Genji.",
        "en": "One interpretation links it with the carriage rivalry in The Tale of Genji."
      }
    }
  ],

      "source_id": "oshira-sama-s1",

  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Otoroshi adalah yōkai yang ditampilkan dalam gulungan dan buku bergambar Jepang. Beberapa nama lain dikaitkan dengan sosok itu. Satu penjelasan menghubungkan namanya dengan kata yang berarti menakutkan.",
      "en": "Otoroshi is a yōkai shown in Japanese scrolls and illustrated books. Several alternate names are associated with the figure. One explanation relates its name to a word for frightening.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "otoroshi-c01",
        "otoroshi-c02",
        "otoroshi-c03"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Usulan asal nama lainnya menghubungkannya dengan rambut yang sangat lebat. Kisah belakangan memberinya tubuh berbulu dengan cakar dan taring. Kisah itu menggambarkannya sebagai penjaga tempat suci.",

  "claims": [

      "id": "otoroshi-c01",
      "source_id": "otoroshi-s1",
      "quote": "Otoroshi (おとろし; \"scary\") are a Japanese yōkai that appear in several yōkai emaki, such as the Hyakkai Zukan by Sawaki Suushi and the Gazu Hyakki Yagyō by Toriyama Sekien (1776).",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Otoroshi adalah yōkai yang ditampilkan dalam gulungan dan buku bergambar Jepang.",
        "en": "Otoroshi is a yōkai shown in Japanese scrolls and illustrated books."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "otoroshi-c02",
      "source_id": "otoroshi-s1",
      "quote": "Other names include Odoro-odoro, Ke Ippai (毛一杯, \"much hair\"), Odoroshi, Odoro-odoroshi, and Otoron.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Beberapa nama lain dikaitkan dengan sosok itu.",
        "en": "Several alternate names are associated with the figure."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "otoroshi-c03",
      "source_id": "otoroshi-s1",
      "quote": "The Otoroshi's name likely comes from a regional corruption of the word osoroshii meaning scary, frightening or disheveled.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "etymology",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Satu penjelasan menghubungkan namanya dengan kata yang berarti menakutkan.",
        "en": "One explanation relates its name to a word for frightening."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "otoroshi-c04",
      "source_id": "otoroshi-s1",
      "quote": "The yōkai researchers Tada and Kenji Murakami posit that the word \"odorogami\" (棘髪), meaning extremely long growths of hair, is another origin for the names.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "etymology",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Usulan asal nama lainnya menghubungkannya dengan rambut yang sangat lebat.",
        "en": "Another proposed origin links the name with heavily grown hair."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "otoroshi-c05",
      "source_id": "otoroshi-s2",
      "quote": "Otoroshi appear as hairy, hunched, four-legged beasts with fierce claws and tusks.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "modern-popular-culture",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kisah belakangan memberinya tubuh berbulu dengan cakar dan taring.",
        "en": "A later account gives it a hairy body with claws and tusks."
      }

    {
      "id": "otoroshi-c06",
      "source_id": "otoroshi-s2",
      "quote": "Interactions : Otoroshi act as a kind of guardian of these holy places.",

      "statement": {

      "id": "Sankai adalah monster kelahiran dalam cerita rakyat Jepang. Mitos menghubungkan kelahirannya dengan kurangnya perawatan selama kehamilan. Versi dari Okayama disebut oketsu.",
      "en": "Sankai is a birth monster in Japanese folklore. The myth connects its birth with insufficient care during pregnancy. An Okayama version is called oketsu.",
      "claim_ids": [

        "sankai-c02",
        "sankai-c03"
      ]

  "claims": [

      "id": "sankai-c01",
      "source_id": "sankai-s1",
      "quote": "In Japanese mythology, a sankai (産怪, sankai; literally, \"birth monster\") is a yōkai (praeternatural creature) that is born to a woman.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Sankai adalah monster kelahiran dalam cerita rakyat Jepang.",
        "en": "Sankai is a birth monster in Japanese folklore."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "sankai-c02",
      "source_id": "sankai-s1",
      "quote": "According to myth, when due care is not given during pregnancy, a sankai would emerge instead.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Mitos menghubungkan kelahirannya dengan kurangnya perawatan selama kehamilan.",
        "en": "The myth connects its birth with insufficient care during pregnancy."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "sankai-c03",
      "source_id": "sankai-s1",
      "quote": "The story of the oketsu (オケツ) comes from Okayama Prefecture.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Versi dari Okayama disebut oketsu.",
        "en": "An Okayama version is called oketsu."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "sankai-c04",
      "source_id": "sankai-s1",
      "quote": "From outer appearance, it looks similar to a turtle, and it has hair growing on its back.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Versi itu menyerupai kura-kura dengan rambut di punggung.",
        "en": "That version resembles a turtle with hair on its back."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "sankai-c05",
      "source_id": "sankai-s1",
      "quote": "As soon as it is born, it starts to crawl on the floor and attempts to escape underneath the house.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ia konon merangkak ke bawah rumah segera setelah lahir.",
        "en": "It is said to crawl beneath the house immediately after birth."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "sankai-c06",
      "source_id": "sankai-s1",
      "quote": "The sankai is known as a kekkai (血塊, ケッカイ) in Saitama Prefecture and Kanagawa Prefecture, and kekke (ケッケ) in Nagano Prefecture.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Wilayah lain memakai nama seperti kekkai dan kekke.",
        "en": "Other regions use names such as kekkai and kekke."

  "short_description": {
    "id": "Shibaemon-tanuki adalah tanuki gaib dari Pulau Awaji.",
    "en": "Shibaemon-tanuki is a supernatural tanuki from Awaji Island.",
    "claim_ids": [
      "shibaemon-tanuki-c01"
    ]
  },
  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Shibaemon-tanuki adalah tanuki gaib dari Pulau Awaji. Ia dihitung sebagai salah satu dari tiga tanuki terkenal Jepang. Kumpulan cerita bergambar zaman Edo mencatat sosoknya.",
      "en": "Shibaemon-tanuki is a supernatural tanuki from Awaji Island. He is counted among Japan’s three famous tanuki. An Edo-period illustrated tale collection records him.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "shibaemon-tanuki-c01",
        "shibaemon-tanuki-c02",
        "shibaemon-tanuki-c03"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Satu cerita menggambarkan anjing membunuhnya ketika ia menyamar sebagai manusia. Satu kisah menyebut istrinya bernama Omasu. Cerita-ceritanya memadukan tipuan dengan bantuan bagi pelancong yang tersesat.",
      "en": "A tale describes a dog killing him while he is disguised as a human. One account names his wife Omasu. His stories combine deceptive tricks with help for lost travelers.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "shibaemon-tanuki-c04",
        "shibaemon-tanuki-c05",
        "shibaemon-tanuki-c06"
      ]
    }
  ],
  "cultural_context": null,

    }
  ],
  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "shibaemon-tanuki-c01",
      "source_id": "shibaemon-tanuki-s1",
      "quote": "Shibaemon-tanuki, or Shibaemon-danuki (芝右衛門狸 or 柴右衛門狸) is a bake-danuki told in the legends of Awaji island, Hyōgo Prefecture.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Shibaemon-tanuki adalah tanuki gaib dari Pulau Awaji.",
        "en": "Shibaemon-tanuki is a supernatural tanuki from Awaji Island."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "shibaemon-tanuki-c02",
      "source_id": "shibaemon-tanuki-s1",
      "quote": "Along with Danzaburou-danuki of Sado island and the Yashima no Hage-tanuki of Kagawa Prefecture, he is counted among the \"three famous tanuki of Japan.\" He is also written about in the colorful stories collection of the Edo period, the Ehon Hyaku Monogatari.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ia dihitung sebagai salah satu dari tiga tanuki terkenal Jepang.",
        "en": "He is counted among Japan’s three famous tanuki."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "shibaemon-tanuki-c03",
      "source_id": "shibaemon-tanuki-s1",
      "quote": "Along with Danzaburou-danuki of Sado island and the Yashima no Hage-tanuki of Kagawa Prefecture, he is counted among the \"three famous tanuki of Japan.\" He is also written about in the colorful stories collection of the Edo period, the Ehon Hyaku Monogatari.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kumpulan cerita bergambar zaman Edo mencatat sosoknya.",
        "en": "An Edo-period illustrated tale collection records him."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "shibaemon-tanuki-c04",
      "source_id": "shibaemon-tanuki-s1",
      "quote": "While disguised as a human and in the middle of performing an act, he was attacked by a dog and killed, but the details of the tale differ depending on region and literature.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Satu cerita menggambarkan anjing membunuhnya ketika ia menyamar sebagai manusia.",
        "en": "A tale describes a dog killing him while he is disguised as a human."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "shibaemon-tanuki-c05",
      "source_id": "shibaemon-tanuki-s1",
      "quote": "Mikuma (三熊山) behind the city of Sumoto, Awaji island, he lived with his wife Omasu (お増) and on moonlight nights with good weather, he drummed his belly.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Satu kisah menyebut istrinya bernama Omasu.",
        "en": "One account names his wife Omasu."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "shibaemon-tanuki-c06",
      "source_id": "shibaemon-tanuki-s1",
      "quote": "He performed mischief such as disguising himself as a human and making tree leaves seem like gold and sell them, but at the same time he did kind deeds such as guiding along humans who stumbled into the mountain while drunk, so nobody hated him.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Cerita-ceritanya memadukan tipuan dengan bantuan bagi pelancong yang tersesat.",
        "en": "His stories combine deceptive tricks with help for lost travelers."
      }
    }
  ],

    ]
  },
  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Shōkera adalah yōkai Jepang yang digambar dalam gulungan zaman Edo. Beberapa ejaan dicatat untuk namanya.",
      "en": "Shōkera is a Japanese yōkai pictured in Edo-period scrolls. Several spellings are recorded for its name.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "shokera-c01",
        "shokera-c02"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Ilustrasi lama tidak memiliki keterangan, sehingga sifatnya tidak pasti. Sekien menggambarnya mengintip melalui jendela atap. Karya belakangan menafsirkannya sebagai pengawasan terhadap kepatuhan pada aturan Kōshin.",
      "en": "The old illustrations lack explanatory text, leaving its nature uncertain. Sekien depicts it peering through a roof skylight. Later literature interprets this as watching whether people observe Kōshin rules.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "shokera-c03",
        "shokera-c04",
        "shokera-c05"
      ]
    }
  ],
  "cultural_context": null,
  "etymology": null,
  "story_mode": null,
  "did_you_know": null,
  "abilities": [],
  "weaknesses": [],

  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "shokera-c01",
      "source_id": "shokera-s1",
      "quote": "The Shōkera (しょうけら; lit. Mole Cricket Spirit) is a Japanese yōkai found in Edo Period yōkai emaki such as, the Hyakkai Zukan and the Gazu Hyakki Yagyō.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Shōkera adalah yōkai Jepang yang digambar dalam gulungan zaman Edo.",
        "en": "Shōkera is a Japanese yōkai pictured in Edo-period scrolls."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "shokera-c02",
      "source_id": "shokera-s1",
      "quote": "They can also be written しゃうけら, せうけら, as well as 精螻蛄.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Beberapa ejaan dicatat untuk namanya.",
        "en": "Several spellings are recorded for its name."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "shokera-c03",
      "source_id": "shokera-s1",
      "quote": "In the Hyakkai Zukan, Gazu Hyakki Yagyō etc., they appear only in pictures with no explanatory text, so what kind of yōkai they are is a matter of speculation.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ilustrasi lama tidak memiliki keterangan, sehingga sifatnya tidak pasti.",
        "en": "The old illustrations lack explanatory text, leaving its nature uncertain."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "shokera-c04",
      "source_id": "shokera-s1",
      "quote": "The Gazu Hyakki Yagyō by Toriyama Sekien depicts a shōkera looking in through the skylight of a house's roof.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Sekien menggambarnya mengintip melalui jendela atap.",
        "en": "Sekien depicts it peering through a roof skylight."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "shokera-c05",
      "source_id": "shokera-s1",
      "quote": "Yōkai-related literature starting in Shōwa and Heisei began to give the interpretation that this is surveillance to see whether people are following the rules of Kōshin-Machi day, and it is supposed that the shōkera would give a punishment by three fingers with sharp nails on those who break the rule.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "modern-popular-culture",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Karya belakangan menafsirkannya sebagai pengawasan terhadap kepatuhan pada aturan Kōshin.",
        "en": "Later literature interprets this as watching whether people observe Kōshin rules."
      }
    }
  ],

  "traits": [],
  "short_description": {
    "id": "Setan cacar dipercaya menyebabkan cacar di Jepang abad pertengahan.",
    "en": "The smallpox demon was believed to cause smallpox in medieval Japan.",
    "claim_ids": [
      "smallpox-demon-c01"
    ]
  },
  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Setan cacar dipercaya menyebabkan cacar di Jepang abad pertengahan. Praktik historis berusaha meredakan amarahnya. Nama Jepangnya dapat diterjemahkan sebagai dewa cacar.",
      "en": "The smallpox demon was believed to cause smallpox in medieval Japan. Historical practices sought to appease its anger. Its Japanese name can be translated as smallpox god.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "smallpox-demon-c01",
        "smallpox-demon-c02",
        "smallpox-demon-c03"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Cerita rakyat menggambarkan roh itu takut pada benda merah dan anjing. Ritual Okinawa memakai musik dan tarian singa di hadapan penderita. Puisi cacar di Ryukyu memuji setan itu.",
      "en": "Folklore described the spirit as afraid of red objects and dogs. Okinawan rites used music and lion dances before a patient. Smallpox poetry in Ryukyu praised the demon.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "smallpox-demon-c04",
        "smallpox-demon-c05",
        "smallpox-demon-c06"
      ]
    }
  ],
  "cultural_context": null,
  "etymology": null,
  "story_mode": null,
  "did_you_know": null,
  "abilities": [],
  "weaknesses": [],
  "variants": [],
  "stories": [],
  "places": [],
  "timeline": [],
  "relations": [],
  "modern_depictions": [],
  "tradition_vs_modern": null,
  "learning_questions": [],
  "images": [],
  "sources": [
    {
      "id": "smallpox-demon-s1",
      "published": null,
      "accessed": "2026-10-01",
      "url": "https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smallpox_demon",
      "title": "Smallpox demon",
      "publisher": "Wikipedia",
      "language": "en",
      "type": "wikipedia",
      "author": null
    }
  ],
  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "smallpox-demon-c01",
      "source_id": "smallpox-demon-s1",
      "quote": "Smallpox demon (Japanese: 疱瘡神, Hōsōgami, Hōsōshin) or smallpox devil is a demon which was believed to be responsible for causing smallpox in medieval Japan.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Setan cacar dipercaya menyebabkan cacar di Jepang abad pertengahan.",
        "en": "The smallpox demon was believed to cause smallpox in medieval Japan."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "smallpox-demon-c02",
      "source_id": "smallpox-demon-s1",
      "quote": "In those days, people tried to appease the smallpox demon by assuaging his anger, or they tried to attack the demon since they had no other effective treatment for smallpox.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Praktik historis berusaha meredakan amarahnya.",
        "en": "Historical practices sought to appease its anger."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "smallpox-demon-c03",
      "source_id": "smallpox-demon-s1",
      "quote": "In Japanese, the word hōsōshin or hōsōgami (疱瘡神 (ほうそうしん, ほうそうがみ）) translates literally to \"smallpox god\".",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "etymology",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Nama Jepangnya dapat diterjemahkan sebagai dewa cacar.",
        "en": "Its Japanese name can be translated as smallpox god."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "smallpox-demon-c04",
      "source_id": "smallpox-demon-s1",
      "quote": "Smallpox devils were said to be afraid of red things and also of dogs; thus people displayed various dolls that were red.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Cerita rakyat menggambarkan roh itu takut pada benda merah dan anjing.",
        "en": "Folklore described the spirit as afraid of red objects and dogs."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "smallpox-demon-c05",
      "source_id": "smallpox-demon-s1",
      "quote": "In Okinawa, they tried to praise and comfort devils with sanshin, an Okinawan musical instrument, and lion dances before a patient clad in red clothes.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ritual Okinawa memakai musik dan tarian singa di hadapan penderita.",
        "en": "Okinawan rites used music and lion dances before a patient."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "smallpox-demon-c06",
      "source_id": "smallpox-demon-s1",
      "quote": "In Okinawa, there was smallpox poetry in Ryuka; the purpose of smallpox poetry in the Ryukyu language is the glorification of the smallpox demon, or improvement from deadly infection of smallpox.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Puisi cacar di Ryukyu memuji setan itu.",
        "en": "Smallpox poetry in Ryukyu praised the demon."
      }
    }
  ],
  "conflicts": [],

      "title": "Spirit turtle",
      "publisher": "Wikipedia",

    }
  ],
  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "spirit-turtle-c01",
      "source_id": "spirit-turtle-s1",
      "quote": "The spirit turtle (Chinese: 靈龜) or spirit tortoise is a turtle originated from Chinese mythology and spread with East Asian cultural sphere.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kura-kura roh adalah makhluk dalam mitologi Tiongkok.",
        "en": "The spirit turtle is a creature of Chinese mythology."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "spirit-turtle-c02",
      "source_id": "spirit-turtle-s1",
      "quote": "It is believed by East Asian cultures, like other turtles in mythology, to represent longevity (壽命).",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ia melambangkan umur panjang dalam tradisi Asia Timur.",
        "en": "It represents longevity in East Asian traditions."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "spirit-turtle-c03",
      "source_id": "spirit-turtle-s1",
      "quote": "It is said to be chief among all shelled creatures.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ia dianggap sebagai pemimpin makhluk bercangkang.",
        "en": "It is regarded as chief of shelled creatures."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "spirit-turtle-c04",
      "source_id": "spirit-turtle-s1",
      "quote": "Among the Four Intelligent Beasts (四靈), a list of auspicious animals, the turtle goes by several names.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ia termasuk dalam Empat Hewan Cerdas.",
        "en": "It belongs to the Four Intelligent Beasts."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "spirit-turtle-c05",
      "source_id": "spirit-turtle-s1",
      "quote": "According to the Shuyiji (述異記 Tales of Strange Matters) compiled by Ren Fang, a turtle that has lived for one thousand years has grown hair, a five thousand year old tortoise is called a \"divine turtle\" (神龜) and an animal older than ten thousand years is called a \"spiritual turtle\" (靈龜).",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Shuyiji menyebut kura-kura yang berusia lebih dari sepuluh ribu tahun sebagai kura-kura roh.",
        "en": "The Shuyiji calls a turtle older than ten thousand years spiritual."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "spirit-turtle-c06",
      "source_id": "spirit-turtle-s1",
      "quote": "The Baopuzi gives a slightly different account, describing the spirit turtle as one thousand years old.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Baopuzi justru menggambarkan kura-kura roh berusia seribu tahun.",
        "en": "The Baopuzi instead describes a thousand-year-old spirit turtle."
      }
    }
  ],

  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Sunekosuri adalah yōkai Jepang yang namanya merujuk pada gesekan pada kaki. Cerita Okayama menggambarkannya menghalangi pejalan kaki. Satu kisah setempat memberinya wujud anjing.",
      "en": "Sunekosuri is a Japanese yōkai whose name refers to rubbing against legs. Okayama tales describe it obstructing pedestrians. One local account gives it a dog’s shape.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "sunekosuri-c01",
        "sunekosuri-c02",
        "sunekosuri-c03"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Ia konon muncul di jalan dan gang pada malam hujan. Gerakannya di antara kaki manusia menyulitkan orang berjalan. Ia tidak digambarkan sengaja mencelakai, meskipun dapat membuat orang tersandung.",
      "en": "It is said to appear on rainy nights in streets and alleys. Its movements between people’s legs make walking difficult. It is not portrayed as deliberately harmful, though it can make people stumble.",

        "sunekosuri-c04",
        "sunekosuri-c05",
        "sunekosuri-c06"
      ]
    }
  ],
  "cultural_context": null,

  ],
  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "sunekosuri-c01",
      "source_id": "sunekosuri-s1",
      "quote": "The sunekosuri (meaning \"rub against lower legs\") is a Japanese yōkai.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Sunekosuri adalah yōkai Jepang yang namanya merujuk pada gesekan pada kaki.",
        "en": "Sunekosuri is a Japanese yōkai whose name refers to rubbing against legs."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "sunekosuri-c02",
      "source_id": "sunekosuri-s1",
      "quote": "Stories are told of it in Okayama Prefecture and is said to get in the way of people's paths as they walk.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Cerita Okayama menggambarkannya menghalangi pejalan kaki.",
        "en": "Okayama tales describe it obstructing pedestrians."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "sunekosuri-c03",
      "source_id": "sunekosuri-s1",
      "quote": "According to folklore from Ibara in Nankaichi town within this city, sunekosuri would appear beside a shrine called Iryō-dō, and it would move through the space between the legs of pedestrians and would take the shape of a dog.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Satu kisah setempat memberinya wujud anjing.",
        "en": "One local account gives it a dog’s shape."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "sunekosuri-c04",
      "source_id": "sunekosuri-s2",
      "quote": "They appear on rainy nights in streets and alleys where people travel.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ia konon muncul di jalan dan gang pada malam hujan.",
        "en": "It is said to appear on rainy nights in streets and alleys."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "sunekosuri-c05",
      "source_id": "sunekosuri-s2",
      "quote": "They rub against their shins, weave in and out of their legs, nuzzle against the knees, and otherwise make it difficult to walk.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Gerakannya di antara kaki manusia menyulitkan orang berjalan.",
        "en": "Its movements between people’s legs make walking difficult."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "sunekosuri-c06",
      "source_id": "sunekosuri-s2",
      "quote": "They do not intentionally cause any harm to humans, although occasionally their rubbing is strong enough to make a person stumble or even knock them down.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ia tidak digambarkan sengaja mencelakai, meskipun dapat membuat orang tersandung.",
        "en": "It is not portrayed as deliberately harmful, though it can make people stumble."
      }
    }
  ],

        "id": "Kojiki menempatkan dewa itu pada urutan kedua dalam Tujuh Generasi.",
        "en": "The Kojiki places the deity second in the Seven Generations."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "toyokumono-no-mikoto-c04",
      "source_id": "toyokumono-no-mikoto-s1",
      "quote": "『古事記』において神世七代の第二代の神とされ、『日本書紀』では天地開闢に登場する神世七代の第三代の神とされる。また神世七代の最後の独神である。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Nihon Shoki menempatkan dewa itu pada urutan ketiga dalam kisah pembukanya.",
        "en": "The Nihon Shoki gives the deity third place in its opening account."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "toyokumono-no-mikoto-c05",
      "source_id": "toyokumono-no-mikoto-s2",
      "quote": "Next was Toyokumono no kami 豊雲野神 (2). These two deities also came into existence as solitary deities and hid their bodies.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kojiki menggambarkan Toyokumono sebagai dewa tunggal.",
        "en": "The Kojiki describes Toyokumono as a solitary deity."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "toyokumono-no-mikoto-c06",
      "source_id": "toyokumono-no-mikoto-s2",
      "quote": "Next was Toyokumono no kami 豊雲野神 (2). These two deities also came into existence as solitary deities and hid their bodies.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kisah tersebut menyebut dewa itu menyembunyikan tubuhnya.",
        "en": "That account says the deity hid its body."
      }
    }
  ],

      "id": "Tsurubebi adalah yōkai api yang digambar oleh Toriyama Sekien. Ilustrasi aslinya tidak memberi keterangan. Satu kisah menggambarkannya sebagai roh pohon malam hari di hutan konifer.",
      "en": "Tsurubebi is a fire yōkai depicted by Toriyama Sekien. The original illustration gives no explanatory text. One account portrays it as a nocturnal tree spirit in coniferous forests.",

        "tsurubebi-c01",
        "tsurubebi-c02",
        "tsurubebi-c03"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Ia muncul sebagai api putih kebiruan yang bergerak antara cabang dan tanah. Wajah manusia atau hewan kadang terlihat di dalam apinya. Apinya digambarkan tidak menghasilkan panas atau merusak pohon.",
      "en": "It appears as bluish-white fire moving between branches and the ground. A human or animal face may be visible in its flames. Its fire is described as producing no heat or damage to trees.",

        "tsurubebi-c05",
        "tsurubebi-c06"

      "id": "tsurubebi-c03",
      "source_id": "tsurubebi-s2",
      "quote": "Tsurubebi are small tree spirits which appear at night, deep in coniferous forests.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Satu kisah menggambarkannya sebagai roh pohon malam hari di hutan konifer.",

      }
    },
    {
      "id": "tsurubebi-c04",
      "source_id": "tsurubebi-s2",
      "quote": "They take the form of blueish-white orbs of fire which bob up and down in the branches, occasionally dropping to the forest floor and floating back up into the trees.",

      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {

        "en": "It appears as bluish-white fire moving between branches and the ground."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "tsurubebi-c05",
      "source_id": "tsurubebi-s2",
      "quote": "Sometimes the vague shape of a human or bestial face can be seen in the flames.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Wajah manusia atau hewan kadang terlihat di dalam apinya.",
        "en": "A human or animal face may be visible in its flames."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "tsurubebi-c06",
      "source_id": "tsurubebi-s2",
      "quote": "Their flames produce no heat and do not burn the trees that they live in; nor do these yokai pose any other known threat.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Apinya digambarkan tidak menghasilkan panas atau merusak pohon.",
        "en": "Its fire is described as producing no heat or damage to trees."
      }

      "en": "Umashiashikabihikoji is a Shinto deity. The Kojiki places his emergence early in the universe’s creation. He is counted as the fourth deity to emerge in that account.",

        "umashi-ashikabi-hikoji-c01",
        "umashi-ashikabi-hikoji-c02",
        "umashi-ashikabi-hikoji-c03"

      "quote": "Umashiashikabihikoji (宇摩志阿斯訶備比古遅; Lit. \"Elderly Prince Reed-Shoot\") is a kami (Shinto deity).",

      "source_id": "umashi-ashikabi-hikoji-s1",

      "source_id": "umashi-ashikabi-hikoji-s1",

      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {

  "short_description": {

    "en": "Yosuzume are bird yōkai known as night sparrows.",
    "claim_ids": [
      "yosuzume-c01"
    ]
  },
  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Yosuzume adalah yōkai burung yang disebut burung pipit malam. Mereka muncul malam hari dan berkicau seperti burung pipit di jalur pegunungan. Satu kepercayaan menyebut penangkapan burung itu menyebabkan rabun senja.",
      "en": "Yosuzume are bird yōkai known as night sparrows. They appear at night and chirp like sparrows on mountain trails. One belief says catching them causes night blindness.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "yosuzume-c01",
        "yosuzume-c02",
        "yosuzume-c03"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Di Wakayama, keberadaannya dapat menandakan serigala pelindung. Kisah lain menafsirkan keberadaan mereka sebagai pertanda sial. Satu kisah daerah menyebut hanya satu pelancong dalam kelompok yang mendengar suaranya.",
      "en": "In Wakayama, their presence can indicate a protective wolf. Other accounts interpret their presence as bad luck. A regional account says only one traveler in a group hears their call.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "yosuzume-c04",
        "yosuzume-c05",
        "yosuzume-c06"
      ]
    }
  ],
  "cultural_context": null,
  "etymology": null,
  "story_mode": null,
  "did_you_know": null,
  "abilities": [],

    {
      "id": "yosuzume-s1",
      "published": null,
      "accessed": "2026-10-01",

      "title": "Yosuzume",
      "publisher": "Wikipedia",

    },
    {
      "id": "yosuzume-s2",
      "published": null,
      "accessed": "2026-10-01",
      "url": "https://yokai.com/yosuzume/",
      "title": "Yosuzume",
      "publisher": "Yokai.com",
      "language": "en",
      "type": "encyclopedia",
      "author": "Matthew Meyer"
    }
  ],
  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "yosuzume-c01",
      "source_id": "yosuzume-s1",
      "quote": "Yosuzume (夜雀, \"Night-Sparrow\") are bird yōkai",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Yosuzume adalah yōkai burung yang disebut burung pipit malam.",
        "en": "Yosuzume are bird yōkai known as night sparrows."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yosuzume-c02",
      "source_id": "yosuzume-s1",
      "quote": "As indicated by their name, they are a yōkai that appear in the night and chirp \"chi, chi, chi\" like a sparrow, and are said to pass in front of people who walk along mountain trails.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Mereka muncul malam hari dan berkicau seperti burung pipit di jalur pegunungan.",
        "en": "They appear at night and chirp like sparrows on mountain trails."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yosuzume-c03",
      "source_id": "yosuzume-s1",
      "quote": "Also, it is said that if one carelessly caught them, then one gets afflicted with nyctalopia.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Satu kepercayaan menyebut penangkapan burung itu menyebabkan rabun senja.",
        "en": "One belief says catching them causes night blindness."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yosuzume-c04",
      "source_id": "yosuzume-s1",
      "quote": "In reverse, in Wakayama Prefecture, they were not bad luck, and while possessed by a night sparrow, it was proof that a wolf was giving protection from the monsters of the mountain.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Di Wakayama, keberadaannya dapat menandakan serigala pelindung.",
        "en": "In Wakayama, their presence can indicate a protective wolf."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yosuzume-c05",
      "source_id": "yosuzume-s2",
      "quote": "By themselves they don’t do any particular harm other than startling people; however they are a sign of very bad luck and are thought to bring terrible evil to those whom they swarm around.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kisah lain menafsirkan keberadaan mereka sebagai pertanda sial.",
        "en": "Other accounts interpret their presence as bad luck."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yosuzume-c06",
      "source_id": "yosuzume-s2",
      "quote": "Their call is mysteriously only ever heard by a single individual, even when traveling in groups.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Satu kisah daerah menyebut hanya satu pelancong dalam kelompok yang mendengar suaranya.",
        "en": "A regional account says only one traveler in a group hears their call."
      }
    }
  ],
  "conflicts": [],
  "gaps": [
    {
      "field": "images",
      "searched": "Belum ada berkas Wikimedia Commons yang diperiksa untuk entri ini."

      "en": "Akago names Japanese yōkai with differing regional and illustrated traditions. A Nagano version lives beneath the water of Lake Kizaki.",

  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "akago-c01",
      "source_id": "akago-s1",
      "quote": "赤子（あかご）は、長野県、大和国（現・奈良県）に伝わる妖怪。または、俳人・与謝蕪村による妖怪絵巻『蕪村妖怪絵巻』にある妖怪。それぞれ伝承が異なる。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Akago adalah nama yōkai Jepang dengan tradisi daerah dan ilustrasi yang berbeda.",
        "en": "Akago names Japanese yōkai with differing regional and illustrated traditions."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "akago-c02",
      "source_id": "akago-s1",
      "quote": "大町市にある仁科三湖のひとつ、木崎湖の水中に住むといわれる。外見は11歳か12歳ほどの人間のようだが、その名が示すように産まれたばかりの赤ん坊のように赤く、髪は猩猩のようだという。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Versi Nagano hidup di dalam air Danau Kizaki.",
        "en": "A Nagano version lives beneath the water of Lake Kizaki."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "akago-c03",
      "source_id": "akago-s1",
      "quote": "外見は11歳か12歳ほどの人間のようだが、その名が示すように産まれたばかりの赤ん坊のように赤く、髪は猩猩のようだという。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ia menyerupai anak dengan kulit merah dan rambut kusut.",
        "en": "It resembles a child with red skin and shaggy hair."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "akago-c04",
      "source_id": "akago-s1",
      "quote": "漁師が水中に隠れている赤子を目撃することがあるものの、特に人間に対して危害を加えることはないという。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Versi danau ini tidak dikatakan mencelakai manusia.",
        "en": "This lake version is not said to harm people."
      }
    }

  },
  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Akaruhime adalah dewi yang disebut namanya dalam Kojiki. Kojiki menjadikannya istri Amenohiboko, pangeran Silla.",
      "en": "Akaruhime is a goddess named in the Kojiki. The Kojiki makes her the wife of Amenohiboko, a Silla prince.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "akaruhime-c01",
        "akaruhime-c02"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Satu cerita menggambarkan permata merah berubah menjadi perempuan muda yang cantik. Setelah suaminya menghinanya, ia melarikan diri dengan perahu ke Naniwa. Narasi mengidentifikasikannya sebagai dewi yang dipuja di Naniwa.",
      "en": "A story describes a red jewel becoming a beautiful maiden. After her husband insults her, she escapes by boat to Naniwa. The narrative identifies her with a goddess enshrined at Naniwa.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "akaruhime-c03",
        "akaruhime-c04",
        "akaruhime-c05"
      ]
    }
  ],
  "cultural_context": null,
  "etymology": null,
  "story_mode": null,

      "id": "akaruhime-s1",

      "title": "阿加流比売神",
      "publisher": "Wikipedia",
      "language": "ja",
      "type": "wikipedia",
      "author": null
    }
  ],
  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "akaruhime-c01",
      "source_id": "akaruhime-s1",
      "quote": "阿加流比売神（アカルヒメノカミ）は、『古事記』にしか登場しない女神の一人。『日本書紀』や『摂津国風土記』にも同一神と思われる似通った説話が記載されているが神名の記述はない。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Akaruhime adalah dewi yang disebut namanya dalam Kojiki.",
        "en": "Akaruhime is a goddess named in the Kojiki."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "akaruhime-c02",
      "source_id": "akaruhime-s1",
      "quote": "『古事記』では新羅王の子である天之日矛（あめのひぼこ）によって一時その正妻とされた。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kojiki menjadikannya istri Amenohiboko, pangeran Silla.",
        "en": "The Kojiki makes her the wife of Amenohiboko, a Silla prince."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "akaruhime-c03",
      "source_id": "akaruhime-s1",
      "quote": "天之日矛がその玉を持ち帰って床に置くと、玉は美しい娘になった。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Satu cerita menggambarkan permata merah berubah menjadi perempuan muda yang cantik.",
        "en": "A story describes a red jewel becoming a beautiful maiden."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "akaruhime-c04",
      "source_id": "akaruhime-s1",
      "quote": "しかし、ある日奢り高ぶった天之日矛が妻を罵ったので、親の国に帰ると言って小舟に乗って難波の津に逃げてきた。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Setelah suaminya menghinanya, ia melarikan diri dengan perahu ke Naniwa.",
        "en": "After her husband insults her, she escapes by boat to Naniwa."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "akaruhime-c05",
      "source_id": "akaruhime-s1",
      "quote": "その娘は、難波の比売碁曾の社に鎮まる阿加流比売神であるという。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Narasi mengidentifikasikannya sebagai dewi yang dipuja di Naniwa.",
        "en": "The narrative identifies her with a goddess enshrined at Naniwa."
      }
    }
  ],

  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Ame-no-Torifune dikaitkan dengan dewa atau kapal ilahi dalam mitos Jepang. Ame-no-Torifune adalah nama lain Tori-no-Iwakusufune. Kojiki menempatkan dewa itu di antara anak Izanaki dan Izanami.",
      "en": "Ame-no-Torifune is associated with a deity or divine vessel in Japanese myth. Ame-no-Torifune is an alternate name of Tori-no-Iwakusufune. The Kojiki places the deity among the offspring of Izanaki and Izanami.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "ame-no-torifune-c01",
        "ame-no-torifune-c02",
        "ame-no-torifune-c03"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Kojiki mengirim dewa itu sebagai pendamping Takemikazuchi dalam misi penyerahan negeri. Dewa itu juga menjadi utusan kepada Kotoshironushi. Komentar akademik menafsirkan dewa itu sebagai personifikasi kapal.",
      "en": "The Kojiki sends the deity as Takemikazuchi’s companion in the land-transfer mission. The deity also serves as messenger to Kotoshironushi. An academic commentary interprets the deity as a personification of ships.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "ame-no-torifune-c04",
        "ame-no-torifune-c05",

  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "ame-no-torifune-c01",
      "source_id": "ame-no-torifune-s1",
      "quote": "鳥之石楠船神（トリノイワクスフネノカミ、歴史的仮名遣：トリノイハクスフネノカミ）は、日本神話に登場する神、または神が乗る船の名前である。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ame-no-Torifune dikaitkan dengan dewa atau kapal ilahi dalam mitos Jepang.",
        "en": "Ame-no-Torifune is associated with a deity or divine vessel in Japanese myth."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ame-no-torifune-c02",
      "source_id": "ame-no-torifune-s1",
      "quote": "別名を天鳥船神（あめのとりふねのかみ）、天鳥船（あめのとりふね）と言い、天夷鳥命と同神とする系図がある。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ame-no-Torifune adalah nama lain Tori-no-Iwakusufune.",
        "en": "Ame-no-Torifune is an alternate name of Tori-no-Iwakusufune."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ame-no-torifune-c03",
      "source_id": "ame-no-torifune-s2",
      "quote": "Next [Izanaki and Izanami] gave birth to a deity whose name is Tori no iwakusufune no kami 鳥之石楠舩神. [This deity’s] other name is Ame no torifune 天鳥舩 (26).",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kojiki menempatkan dewa itu di antara anak Izanaki dan Izanami.",
        "en": "The Kojiki places the deity among the offspring of Izanaki and Izanami."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ame-no-torifune-c04",
      "source_id": "ame-no-torifune-s1",
      "quote": "『古事記』の葦原中国平定の段では、天鳥船神が建御雷神の副使として葦原中国に派遣され、事代主神の意見をきくために使者として遣わされた。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kojiki mengirim dewa itu sebagai pendamping Takemikazuchi dalam misi penyerahan negeri.",
        "en": "The Kojiki sends the deity as Takemikazuchi’s companion in the land-transfer mission."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ame-no-torifune-c05",
      "source_id": "ame-no-torifune-s1",
      "quote": "『古事記』の葦原中国平定の段では、天鳥船神が建御雷神の副使として葦原中国に派遣され、事代主神の意見をきくために使者として遣わされた。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Dewa itu juga menjadi utusan kepada Kotoshironushi.",
        "en": "The deity also serves as messenger to Kotoshironushi."
      }

      "id": "ame-no-torifune-c06",

  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Amenotokotachi adalah salah satu dewa langit awal dalam mitologi Jepang. Kojiki menempatkan dewa ini terakhir di antara lima dewa langit pertama. Narasi awal menggambarkan Amenotokotachi sebagai dewa tunggal.",
      "en": "Amenotokotachi is one of the primordial heavenly deities in Japanese mythology. The Kojiki places this deity last among the first five heavenly deities. The early narrative describes Amenotokotachi as a solitary deity.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "amenotokotachi-c01",
        "amenotokotachi-c02",
        "amenotokotachi-c03"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Narasi tersebut menyebut dewa itu menyembunyikan tubuhnya. Dewa itu muncul dalam versi alternatif Nihon Shoki, bukan kisah utamanya. Versi tersebut menempatkan dewa itu sebelum Umashiashikabihikoji dan Kuninotokotachi.",
      "en": "That narrative says the deity hid its body. The deity appears in a Nihon Shoki variant rather than its main account. That variant places the deity before Umashiashikabihikoji and Kuninotokotachi.",

        "amenotokotachi-c05",

      "title": "01-Studies-on-the-Kojiki-Chapter-1-Heaven-and-Earth-First-Become-Active.pdf",
      "publisher": "Kokugakuin University",
      "author": null,
      "language": "en",
      "type": "primary-text"
    }
  ],
  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "amenotokotachi-c01",
      "source_id": "amenotokotachi-s1",
      "quote": "天之常立神（アメノトコタチ）は、日本神話に登場する神。別天津神のうちの一柱である。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Amenotokotachi adalah salah satu dewa langit awal dalam mitologi Jepang.",
        "en": "Amenotokotachi is one of the primordial heavenly deities in Japanese mythology."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "amenotokotachi-c02",
      "source_id": "amenotokotachi-s1",
      "quote": "天地開闢の際、別天津神五柱の最後に現れた神である。独神であり、現れてすぐに身を隠した。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kojiki menempatkan dewa ini terakhir di antara lima dewa langit pertama.",
        "en": "The Kojiki places this deity last among the first five heavenly deities."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "amenotokotachi-c03",
      "source_id": "amenotokotachi-s2",
      "quote": "Next Ame no tokotachi no kami 天之常立神 (9) [appeared]. These two deities also came into existence as solitary deities and hid their bodies.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Narasi awal menggambarkan Amenotokotachi sebagai dewa tunggal.",
        "en": "The early narrative describes Amenotokotachi as a solitary deity."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "amenotokotachi-c04",
      "source_id": "amenotokotachi-s2",
      "quote": "Next Ame no tokotachi no kami 天之常立神 (9) [appeared]. These two deities also came into existence as solitary deities and hid their bodies.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Narasi tersebut menyebut dewa itu menyembunyikan tubuhnya.",
        "en": "That narrative says the deity hid its body."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "amenotokotachi-c05",
      "source_id": "amenotokotachi-s1",
      "quote": "『日本書紀』正伝には現れず、『古事記』および『日本書紀』の異伝（一書）にのみ登場する。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Dewa itu muncul dalam versi alternatif Nihon Shoki, bukan kisah utamanya.",
        "en": "The deity appears in a Nihon Shoki variant rather than its main account."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "amenotokotachi-c06",
      "source_id": "amenotokotachi-s1",
      "quote": "神代紀第一段第六の一書では天常立尊と表記され、可美葦牙彦舅尊（うましあしかびひこぢ）・国之常立神（くにのとこたちのかみ）に先立って最初に登場する。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Versi tersebut menempatkan dewa itu sebelum Umashiashikabihikoji dan Kuninotokotachi.",
        "en": "That variant places the deity before Umashiashikabihikoji and Kuninotokotachi."
      }
    }
  ],

      "id": "Bakotsu adalah yōkai kuda yang digambar dalam Tosa Obake Zōshi zaman Edo. Namanya secara harfiah berarti tulang kuda. Ia konon muncul dari kuda yang mati dalam kebakaran.",
      "en": "Bakotsu is a horse yōkai pictured in the Edo-period Tosa Obake Zōshi. Its name literally means horse bone. It is said to arise from a horse that dies in a fire.",

        "bakotsu-c02",
        "bakotsu-c03"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Satu kisah memberinya pakaian dan posisi duduk seperti manusia. Kisah itu menggambarkannya menghantui bekas pemilik kuda. Gulungan itu menggambarkan dua yōkai bersama di bawah kelambu.",

  "countries": null,

  "traits": [],
  "short_description": {
    "id": "Furutsubaki-no-rei adalah yōkai yang dikaitkan dengan pohon kamelia tua.",
    "en": "Furutsubaki-no-rei is a yōkai associated with old camellia trees.",
    "claim_ids": [
      "furutsubaki-no-rei-c01"
    ]
  },
  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Furutsubaki-no-rei adalah yōkai yang dikaitkan dengan pohon kamelia tua. Sekien menggambarkan kamelia tua yang dihuni roh penipu. Bunga pohon itu jatuh utuh, bukan helai demi helai.",
      "en": "Furutsubaki-no-rei is a yōkai associated with old camellia trees. Sekien describes an old camellia inhabited by a deceptive spirit. The tree’s flowers fall whole rather than petal by petal.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "furutsubaki-no-rei-c01",
        "furutsubaki-no-rei-c02",
        "furutsubaki-no-rei-c03"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Ciri tersebut dikaitkan dengan kematian dalam tradisi Jepang. Cerita Yamagata menggambarkan perempuan cantik mengubah seorang pedagang menjadi lebah. Cerita Akita menafsirkan tangisan pohon sebagai peringatan bencana.",
      "en": "This feature is associated with death in Japanese tradition. A Yamagata tale describes a beautiful woman turning a merchant into a bee. An Akita tale interprets the tree’s crying as a warning of misfortune.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "furutsubaki-no-rei-c04",
        "furutsubaki-no-rei-c05",
        "furutsubaki-no-rei-c06"
      ]
    }
  ],
  "cultural_context": null,
  "etymology": null,
  "story_mode": null,
  "did_you_know": null,
  "abilities": [],

    {
      "id": "furutsubaki-no-rei-s2",
      "published": null,

      "url": "https://yokai.com/furutsubakinorei/",
      "title": "Furutsubaki no rei",
      "publisher": "Yokai.com",
      "language": "en",
      "type": "encyclopedia",
      "author": "Matthew Meyer"
    }
  ],
  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "furutsubaki-no-rei-c01",
      "source_id": "furutsubaki-no-rei-s1",
      "quote": "The Furutsubaki-no-rei (Japanese: 古椿の霊, \"old camellia spirit\") is a yōkai said to inhabit and develop from old camellia trees.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Furutsubaki-no-rei adalah yōkai yang dikaitkan dengan pohon kamelia tua.",
        "en": "Furutsubaki-no-rei is a yōkai associated with old camellia trees."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "furutsubaki-no-rei-c02",
      "source_id": "furutsubaki-no-rei-s1",
      "quote": "According to Toriyama Sekien's “Konjaku Gazu Zoku Hyakki”, a spirit dwells in an old camellia tree, turning into an apparition tree to fool people.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Sekien menggambarkan kamelia tua yang dihuni roh penipu.",
        "en": "Sekien describes an old camellia inhabited by a deceptive spirit."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "furutsubaki-no-rei-c03",
      "source_id": "furutsubaki-no-rei-s2",
      "quote": "The tsubaki is an evergreen tree which has the strange behavior of not losing its flowers gradually, petal by petal, but dropping them all at once to the ground.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Bunga pohon itu jatuh utuh, bukan helai demi helai.",
        "en": "The tree’s flowers fall whole rather than petal by petal."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "furutsubaki-no-rei-c04",
      "source_id": "furutsubaki-no-rei-s2",
      "quote": "As a result, it long been associated with death and strangeness in Japan (and is also taboo to bring as gifts to hospitals or sick people).",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ciri tersebut dikaitkan dengan kematian dalam tradisi Jepang.",
        "en": "This feature is associated with death in Japanese tradition."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "furutsubaki-no-rei-c05",
      "source_id": "furutsubaki-no-rei-s2",
      "quote": "Suddenly a beautiful young woman appeared from out of nowhere on the road beside one of the merchants. She breathed on him, and instantly he transformed into a bee.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Cerita Yamagata menggambarkan perempuan cantik mengubah seorang pedagang menjadi lebah.",
        "en": "A Yamagata tale describes a beautiful woman turning a merchant into a bee."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "furutsubaki-no-rei-c06",
      "source_id": "furutsubaki-no-rei-s2",
      "quote": "This happened again and again, and soon the priests at the temple realized that the tsubaki would cry a warning every time something bad was going to happen.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Cerita Akita menafsirkan tangisan pohon sebagai peringatan bencana.",
        "en": "An Akita tale interprets the tree’s crying as a warning of misfortune."
      }
    }
  ],

  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Hayaakitsuhiko dan Hayaakitsuhime adalah pasangan dewa Jepang. Pasangan itu lahir dari Izanagi dan Izanami. Kojiki menghubungkan pasangan itu dengan muara.",
      "en": "Hayaakitsuhiko and Hayaakitsuhime are a pair of Japanese deities. The pair is born to Izanagi and Izanami. The Kojiki associates the pair with inlets.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "hayaakitsuhiko-and-hayaakitsuhime-c01",
        "hayaakitsuhiko-and-hayaakitsuhime-c02",
        "hayaakitsuhiko-and-hayaakitsuhime-c03"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Mereka membagi tanggung jawab atas sungai dan laut. Kojiki menyebut mereka memiliki delapan anak yang berkaitan dengan air. Komentar menjelaskan minato sebagai bukaan tempat air mengalir masuk dan keluar.",

  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "hayaakitsuhiko-and-hayaakitsuhime-c01",
      "source_id": "hayaakitsuhiko-and-hayaakitsuhime-s1",
      "quote": "『古事記』では速秋津比古神・速秋津比売神、『日本書紀』では速秋津日命（はやあきつひのみこと）と表記される。古事記では別名水戸神（みなとのかみ）と記している。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Hayaakitsuhiko dan Hayaakitsuhime adalah pasangan dewa Jepang.",
        "en": "Hayaakitsuhiko and Hayaakitsuhime are a pair of Japanese deities."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "hayaakitsuhiko-and-hayaakitsuhime-c02",
      "source_id": "hayaakitsuhiko-and-hayaakitsuhime-s1",
      "quote": "神産みの段で伊邪那岐命・伊邪那美命二神の間に産まれた男女一対の神で、水戸神はその総称である。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Pasangan itu lahir dari Izanagi dan Izanami.",
        "en": "The pair is born to Izanagi and Izanami."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "hayaakitsuhiko-and-hayaakitsuhime-c03",
      "source_id": "hayaakitsuhiko-and-hayaakitsuhime-s2",
      "quote": "Next they bore the deity of the inlets (8), whose name is Haya akitsuhiko no kami 速秋津日 子神, and next his sister-spouse Haya akitsuhime no kami 速秋津比売神 (9).",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kojiki menghubungkan pasangan itu dengan muara.",
        "en": "The Kojiki associates the pair with inlets."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "hayaakitsuhiko-and-hayaakitsuhime-c04",
      "source_id": "hayaakitsuhiko-and-hayaakitsuhime-s2",
      "quote": "These two deities, Haya akitsuhiko no kami and Haya akitsuhime no kami, divided between themselves charge of the rivers and the seas (10).",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Mereka membagi tanggung jawab atas sungai dan laut.",
        "en": "They divide responsibility for rivers and seas."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "hayaakitsuhiko-and-hayaakitsuhime-c05",
      "source_id": "hayaakitsuhiko-and-hayaakitsuhime-s1",
      "quote": "『古事記』では、二神の間には以下の四対八柱の神が産まれたと記している。いずれも水に関係のある神である。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kojiki menyebut mereka memiliki delapan anak yang berkaitan dengan air.",
        "en": "The Kojiki attributes eight water-related offspring to them."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "hayaakitsuhiko-and-hayaakitsuhime-c06",
      "source_id": "hayaakitsuhiko-and-hayaakitsuhime-s2",
      "quote": "“Deity of the inlets” (minato no kami 水戸神) Minato conveys the idea of a portal through which water flows in and out.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "etymology",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Komentar menjelaskan minato sebagai bukaan tempat air mengalir masuk dan keluar.",

      }

    ]
  },
  "countries": null,
  "era": null,
  "habitats": [],
  "disposition": null,
  "traits": [],
  "short_description": {
    "id": "Ikuchi adalah yōkai ular laut dalam legenda Jepang.",
    "en": "Ikuchi is a sea-serpent yōkai in Japanese legend.",
    "claim_ids": [
      "ikuchi-c01"
    ]
  },
  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Ikuchi adalah yōkai ular laut dalam legenda Jepang. Kumpulan anekdot zaman Edo menggambarkan makhluk itu. Kisah Tankai menempatkannya di perairan dalam lepas Hitachi.",
      "en": "Ikuchi is a sea-serpent yōkai in Japanese legend. Edo-period anecdote collections describe the creature. The Tankai account places it in deep water off Hitachi.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "ikuchi-c01",
        "ikuchi-c02",
        "ikuchi-c03"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Ia melintas di atas kapal dan meninggalkan minyak kental. Pelaut dalam cerita harus membuang minyak itu agar kapal tidak tenggelam. Panjangnya yang luar biasa membuat lintasannya di atas kapal berlangsung berjam-jam.",
      "en": "It crosses over ships and leaves viscous oil behind. Sailors in the story must remove the oil to keep the ship from sinking. Its extreme length makes a crossing over a ship last for hours.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "ikuchi-c04",
        "ikuchi-c05",
        "ikuchi-c06"
      ]
    }
  ],
  "cultural_context": null,
  "etymology": null,
  "story_mode": null,
  "did_you_know": null,
  "abilities": [],
  "weaknesses": [],
  "variants": [],
  "stories": [],
  "places": [],
  "timeline": [],
  "relations": [],
  "modern_depictions": [],
  "tradition_vs_modern": null,
  "learning_questions": [],
  "images": [],
  "sources": [
    {
      "id": "ikuchi-s1",
      "published": null,
      "accessed": "2026-10-01",
      "url": "https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ikuchi",
      "title": "Ikuchi",
      "publisher": "Wikipedia",

      "author": null
    },
    {
      "id": "ikuchi-s2",
      "published": null,
      "accessed": "2026-10-01",
      "url": "https://yokai.com/ikuchi/",
      "title": "Ikuchi",
      "publisher": "Yokai.com",
      "language": "en",
      "type": "encyclopedia",
      "author": "Matthew Meyer"
    }
  ],
  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "ikuchi-c01",
      "source_id": "ikuchi-s1",
      "quote": "Ikuchi is a yōkai of the sea serpent type in Japanese legend.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ikuchi adalah yōkai ular laut dalam legenda Jepang.",
        "en": "Ikuchi is a sea-serpent yōkai in Japanese legend."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ikuchi-c02",
      "source_id": "ikuchi-s1",
      "quote": "It has been described in two anecdote collections during the Edo period, namely Tankai (譚海) (1795) by Tsumura Sōan and Mimibukuro (耳嚢) (completed 1814) by Negishi Yasumori .",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kumpulan anekdot zaman Edo menggambarkan makhluk itu.",
        "en": "Edo-period anecdote collections describe the creature."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ikuchi-c03",
      "source_id": "ikuchi-s1",
      "quote": "According to Tankai (\"Sea of Stories\", 1795) by Tsumura Sōan, the ikuchi (ゐくち) is an extremely long fish dwelling in the deep waters off Hitachi Province (now Ibaraki Prefecture).",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kisah Tankai menempatkannya di perairan dalam lepas Hitachi.",
        "en": "The Tankai account places it in deep water off Hitachi."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ikuchi-c04",
      "source_id": "ikuchi-s1",
      "quote": "It has only been spotted at night, sometimes rearing out of water and slinking over a ship, taking a long time to complete its passage, and leaving a residue of viscous oil.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ia melintas di atas kapal dan meninggalkan minyak kental.",
        "en": "It crosses over ships and leaves viscous oil behind."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ikuchi-c05",
      "source_id": "ikuchi-s1",
      "quote": "The sheer amount of oil needs to be dumped overboard lest the ship sink.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Pelaut dalam cerita harus membuang minyak itu agar kapal tidak tenggelam.",
        "en": "Sailors in the story must remove the oil to keep the ship from sinking."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ikuchi-c06",
      "source_id": "ikuchi-s2",
      "quote": "Ikuchi are so long—many kilometers, by some accounts—that it can take hours for an entire one to slither over a boat.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Panjangnya yang luar biasa membuat lintasannya di atas kapal berlangsung berjam-jam.",
        "en": "Its extreme length makes a crossing over a ship last for hours."
      }
    }
  ],
  "conflicts": [],
  "gaps": [
    {
      "field": "images",
      "searched": "Belum ada berkas Wikimedia Commons yang diperiksa untuk entri ini."
