  "short_description": {
    "id": "Ikutsuhikone adalah dewa dalam mitologi Jepang.",
    "en": "Ikutsuhikone is a deity in Japanese mythology.",
    "claim_ids": [
      "ikutsuhikone-c01"
    ]
  },
  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Ikutsuhikone adalah dewa dalam mitologi Jepang. Ia dianggap sebagai putra keempat Amaterasu. Satu kisah melahirkannya dari permata Amaterasu yang dikunyah Susanoo.",
      "en": "Ikutsuhikone is a deity in Japanese mythology. He is regarded as Amaterasu’s fourth son. One account produces him from Amaterasu’s jewels chewed by Susanoo.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "ikutsuhikone-c01",
        "ikutsuhikone-c02",
        "ikutsuhikone-c03"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Kisah lain justru menyebutnya sebagai putra Susanoo. Kelahirannya merupakan bagian dari perlombaan antara kedua dewa. Kojiki menghubungkan kemunculannya dengan untaian permata di pergelangan kiri Amaterasu.",
      "en": "Another account instead calls him a son of Susanoo. His birth belongs to a contest between the two deities. The Kojiki connects his emergence with the jeweled cord on Amaterasu’s left wrist.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "ikutsuhikone-c04",
        "ikutsuhikone-c05",
        "ikutsuhikone-c06"
      ]
    }
  ],
  "cultural_context": null,
  "etymology": null,
  "story_mode": null,
  "did_you_know": null,
  "abilities": [],

    }
  ],
  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "ikutsuhikone-c01",
      "source_id": "ikutsuhikone-s1",
      "quote": "Ikutsuhikone (Japanese: イクツヒコネ,活津日子根命/活津彦根命) is a god of Japanese mythology.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ikutsuhikone adalah dewa dalam mitologi Jepang.",
        "en": "Ikutsuhikone is a deity in Japanese mythology."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ikutsuhikone-c02",
      "source_id": "ikutsuhikone-s1",
      "quote": "He is the fourth son of Amaterasu.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ia dianggap sebagai putra keempat Amaterasu.",
        "en": "He is regarded as Amaterasu’s fourth son."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ikutsuhikone-c03",
      "source_id": "ikutsuhikone-s1",
      "quote": "In many versions of his birth story, he is born from Amaterasu’s jewels after being chewed up and spat out by Susanoo-no-Mikoto.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Satu kisah melahirkannya dari permata Amaterasu yang dikunyah Susanoo.",
        "en": "One account produces him from Amaterasu’s jewels chewed by Susanoo."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ikutsuhikone-c04",
      "source_id": "ikutsuhikone-s1",
      "quote": "In another version he comes from Susanoo-no-Mikoto's jewels directly and is thus his son.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kisah lain justru menyebutnya sebagai putra Susanoo.",
        "en": "Another account instead calls him a son of Susanoo."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ikutsuhikone-c05",
      "source_id": "ikutsuhikone-s1",
      "quote": "He was born out of a kami-making competition between Amaterasu and Susanoo.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kelahirannya merupakan bagian dari perlombaan antara kedua dewa.",
        "en": "His birth belongs to a contest between the two deities."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ikutsuhikone-c06",
      "source_id": "ikutsuhikone-s2",
      "quote": "Then he asked for the jeweled [cord] adorning her left wrist, and [she] handed it over [to him]. He chewed it up and spat the bits out in a misty spray from which came into existence a deity named Ikutsuhikone no mikoto 活津日子根命 (14).",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kojiki menghubungkan kemunculannya dengan untaian permata di pergelangan kiri Amaterasu.",
        "en": "The Kojiki connects his emergence with the jeweled cord on Amaterasu’s left wrist."
      }
    }
  ],

  "short_description": {

    "en": "Isetsuhiko is a Japanese wind deity known from provincial Fudoki accounts.",
    "claim_ids": [
      "isetsuhiko-c01"
    ]
  },
  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Isetsuhiko adalah dewa angin Jepang yang dikenal dari kisah Fudoki provinsi. Satu cerita mengisahkan penyerahannya atas Ise kepada Amenohiwake.",
      "en": "Isetsuhiko is a Japanese wind deity known from provincial Fudoki accounts. One tale recounts his surrender of Ise to Amenohiwake.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "isetsuhiko-c01",
        "isetsuhiko-c02"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Ia berjanji pergi ke timur melintasi laut di atas ombak yang digerakkan angin. Cerita lain menggambarkan benteng batu yang gagal ditaklukkan Abeshihiko. Sebagian sarjana mengusulkan penyamaan Isetsuhiko dengan Takeminakata.",
      "en": "He promises to depart eastward over the sea on wind-driven waves. Another tale describes a stone fortress that Abeshihiko fails to conquer. Some scholars have proposed identifying Isetsuhiko with Takeminakata.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "isetsuhiko-c03",
        "isetsuhiko-c04",
        "isetsuhiko-c05"
      ]
    }
  ],
  "cultural_context": null,
  "etymology": null,
  "story_mode": null,
  "did_you_know": null,
  "abilities": [],
  "weaknesses": [],
  "variants": [],
  "stories": [],
  "places": [],
  "timeline": [],
  "relations": [],
  "modern_depictions": [],
  "tradition_vs_modern": null,
  "learning_questions": [],
  "images": [],
  "sources": [
    {
      "id": "isetsuhiko-s1",
      "published": null,
      "accessed": "2026-10-01",
      "url": "https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isetsuhiko",
      "title": "Isetsuhiko",
      "publisher": "Wikipedia",
      "language": "en",

      "author": null
    }
  ],
  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "isetsuhiko-c01",
      "source_id": "isetsuhiko-s1",
      "quote": "Isetsuhiko-no-mikoto (伊勢都彦命 or 伊勢都比古命) is an obscure Japanese god of the wind who appears in both the Fudoki of Ise Province (surviving only in the form of excerpts found in other writings) and the Fudoki of Harima Province.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Isetsuhiko adalah dewa angin Jepang yang dikenal dari kisah Fudoki provinsi.",
        "en": "Isetsuhiko is a Japanese wind deity known from provincial Fudoki accounts."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "isetsuhiko-c02",
      "source_id": "isetsuhiko-s1",
      "quote": "One excerpt involves his surrender of his land (what would become Ise) to Amenohiwake-no-mikoto (天日別命), who claimed it in the name of the Emperor Jimmu.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Satu cerita mengisahkan penyerahannya atas Ise kepada Amenohiwake.",
        "en": "One tale recounts his surrender of Ise to Amenohiwake."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "isetsuhiko-c03",
      "source_id": "isetsuhiko-s1",
      "quote": "[Isetsuhiko] said, 'Tonight I shall cause the eight winds to blow over the waters of the sea and ride the waves to go east.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ia berjanji pergi ke timur melintasi laut di atas ombak yang digerakkan angin.",
        "en": "He promises to depart eastward over the sea on wind-driven waves."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "isetsuhiko-c04",
      "source_id": "isetsuhiko-s1",
      "quote": "Long ago, this god made a fortress out of stone and dwelt therein. Abeshihiko-no-kami (阿倍志彦神) came [to conquer it], but he did not prevail and so retreated.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Cerita lain menggambarkan benteng batu yang gagal ditaklukkan Abeshihiko.",
        "en": "Another tale describes a stone fortress that Abeshihiko fails to conquer."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "isetsuhiko-c05",
      "source_id": "isetsuhiko-s1",
      "quote": "Since the Edo period, a number of authors - among them the Kokugaku scholar Motoori Norinaga - have attempted to identify Isetsuhiko with the god Takeminakata",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "scholarly-interpretation",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Sebagian sarjana mengusulkan penyamaan Isetsuhiko dengan Takeminakata.",
        "en": "Some scholars have proposed identifying Isetsuhiko with Takeminakata."
      }
    }
  ],
  "conflicts": [],

    {

      "id": "kakuen-c01",
      "source_id": "kakuen-s1",
      "quote": "Jué yuán (Chinese and Japanese: 玃猿; pinyin: Jué yuán; rōmaji: Kakuen) is a legendary animal in the legends of China.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kakuen adalah nama Jepang bagi hewan legenda dalam tradisi Tiongkok.",
        "en": "Kakuen is the Japanese name for a legendary animal in Chinese lore."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kakuen-c02",
      "source_id": "kakuen-s1",
      "quote": "They are also called Jué (玃) (in Japanese kaku or ōzaru), Jué fù (玃父), Jiā (猳), Jiā guó (猳國) (in Japanese kakoku カ国), and mǎ huà (馬化) (in Japanese baka).",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Makhluk itu memiliki beberapa nama lain dalam bahasa Tionghoa dan Jepang.",
        "en": "The creature has several Chinese and Japanese alternate names."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kakuen-c03",
      "source_id": "kakuen-s1",
      "quote": "They are similar to monkeys, and thus possess a characteristic of carrying away human females and violating them.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Cerita menggambarkannya menyerupai kera dan menculik perempuan.",
        "en": "Stories portray it as ape-like and abducting women."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kakuen-c04",
      "source_id": "kakuen-s1",
      "quote": "According to the medical herb book Bencao Gangmu (本草綱目), they are bigger than monkeys, and according to the Baopuzi (抱朴子), a míhóu (獼猴; \"rhesus monkey\")) that lives for 800 years becomes a yuán (猨; \"ape\"), and if they live for 500 more years, then they become a jué, which can live 1000 more years.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Bencao Gangmu menggambarkannya lebih besar daripada monyet.",
        "en": "The Bencao Gangmu describes it as larger than a monkey."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kakuen-c05",
      "source_id": "kakuen-s1",
      "quote": "A \"jué\" is a monkey that has grown old, and has a dark blue color.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Gambaran lain memberi makhluk tua itu warna biru gelap.",
        "en": "Another description gives the aged creature a dark blue colour."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kakuen-c06",
      "source_id": "kakuen-s1",
      "quote": "It walks like a human, and they often kidnap humans or objects.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ia berjalan seperti manusia dan membawa pergi manusia atau benda.",
        "en": "It walks like a human and carries away people or objects."
      }
    }
  ],
  "conflicts": [],
  "gaps": [
    {
      "field": "images",

    }

}

    "id": "Kameosa adalah yōkai Jepang yang digambar dalam Hyakki Tsurezure Bukuro karya Sekien.",

    "claim_ids": [

    ]
  },
  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Kameosa adalah yōkai Jepang yang digambar dalam Hyakki Tsurezure Bukuro karya Sekien. Sekien menggambar tempayan air dengan mata, hidung, dan mulut. Keterangannya memberi tempayan itu persediaan air yang tidak habis.",
      "en": "Kameosa is a Japanese yōkai depicted in Sekien’s Hyakki Tsurezure Bukuro. Sekien depicts a water jar with eyes, nose and mouth. His description gives the jar an inexhaustible supply of water.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "kameosa-c01",
        "kameosa-c02",
        "kameosa-c03"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Kisah belakangan menafsirkannya sebagai tempayan tua yang berubah menjadi tsukumogami. Kisah tersebut memperluas isi yang tidak habis itu menjadi air atau sake. Rujukan modern menggambarkan sosok itu sebagai ciptaan Sekien.",
      "en": "A later account interprets it as an old jar transformed into a tsukumogami. That account extends its endless contents to water or sake. A modern reference describes the figure as Sekien’s invention.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "kameosa-c04",
        "kameosa-c05",
        "kameosa-c06"
      ]
    }
  ],
  "cultural_context": null,
  "etymology": null,
  "story_mode": null,
  "did_you_know": null,

  "claims": [

      "id": "kameosa-c01",
      "source_id": "kameosa-s1",
      "quote": "甌長、瓶長、甕長（かめおさ）は、鳥山石燕の妖怪画集『百器徒然袋』にある日本の妖怪。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kameosa adalah yōkai Jepang yang digambar dalam Hyakki Tsurezure Bukuro karya Sekien.",
        "en": "Kameosa is a Japanese yōkai depicted in Sekien’s Hyakki Tsurezure Bukuro."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kameosa-c02",
      "source_id": "kameosa-s1",
      "quote": "水瓶が目鼻と口を備えたような姿で描かれており、石燕の解説文によれば、瓶の水を汲んでも決して水が尽きることのない、いわば幸せの入った瓶として、以下のように述べられている。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Sekien menggambar tempayan air dengan mata, hidung, dan mulut.",
        "en": "Sekien depicts a water jar with eyes, nose and mouth."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kameosa-c03",
      "source_id": "kameosa-s1",
      "quote": "水瓶が目鼻と口を備えたような姿で描かれており、石燕の解説文によれば、瓶の水を汲んでも決して水が尽きることのない、いわば幸せの入った瓶として、以下のように述べられている。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Keterangannya memberi tempayan itu persediaan air yang tidak habis.",
        "en": "His description gives the jar an inexhaustible supply of water."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kameosa-c04",
      "source_id": "kameosa-s2",
      "quote": "Kameosa are old earthenware jugs used to hold water or sake that have changed into tsukumogami—objects that have been around for so long that they develop a spirit.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "modern-popular-culture",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kisah belakangan menafsirkannya sebagai tempayan tua yang berubah menjadi tsukumogami.",
        "en": "A later account interprets it as an old jar transformed into a tsukumogami."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kameosa-c05",
      "source_id": "kameosa-s2",
      "quote": "They can serve up an endless supply of water or sake—whatever they hold.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "modern-popular-culture",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kisah tersebut memperluas isi yang tidak habis itu menjadi air atau sake.",
        "en": "That account extends its endless contents to water or sake."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kameosa-c06",
      "source_id": "kameosa-s2",
      "quote": "Kameosa was invented by Toriyama Sekien for his fourth and final yōkai encyclopedia, Hyakki tsurezure bukuro —a book full of tsukumogami based on puns.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "scholarly-interpretation",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Rujukan modern menggambarkan sosok itu sebagai ciptaan Sekien.",

      }

    "en": "Kamikiri is a Japanese yōkai said to cut people’s hair secretly.",
    "claim_ids": [

    ]
  },
  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Kamikiri adalah yōkai Jepang yang konon memotong rambut manusia diam-diam. Kisahnya dikaitkan dengan desas-desus perkotaan zaman Edo. Korban konon tidak menyadari ketika rambutnya dipotong.",
      "en": "Kamikiri is a Japanese yōkai said to cut people’s hair secretly. Its stories are linked to urban rumours of the Edo period. Victims are said not to notice when their hair is cut.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "kamikiri-haircutting-c01",
        "kamikiri-haircutting-c02",
        "kamikiri-haircutting-c03"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Laporan surat kabar tahun 1874 menggambarkan kejadian yang melibatkan pelayan bernama Gin. Satu kisah menggambarkan sasaran dari berbagai jenis kelamin dan lapisan sosial. Ia konon mengintai di gang dan kamar mandi.",
      "en": "An 1874 newspaper report described an incident involving a servant named Gin. One account describes targets across sexes and social ranks. It is said to stalk alleys and bathrooms.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "kamikiri-haircutting-c04",
        "kamikiri-haircutting-c05",
        "kamikiri-haircutting-c06"
      ]
    }
  ],

  ],
  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "kamikiri-haircutting-c01",
      "source_id": "kamikiri-haircutting-s1",
      "quote": "Kamikiri (髪切り, hair-cutter) or Kurokamikiri (黒髪切, black hair-cutter) is a Japanese yōkai said to secretly cut the hair of people's head.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kamikiri adalah yōkai Jepang yang konon memotong rambut manusia diam-diam.",
        "en": "Kamikiri is a Japanese yōkai said to cut people’s hair secretly."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kamikiri-haircutting-c02",
      "source_id": "kamikiri-haircutting-s1",
      "quote": "The origins of the kamikiri can be traced to urban rumors of the Edo period; incidents attributed to them were intermittently recorded from the 17th to 19th centuries.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kisahnya dikaitkan dengan desas-desus perkotaan zaman Edo.",
        "en": "Its stories are linked to urban rumours of the Edo period."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kamikiri-haircutting-c03",
      "source_id": "kamikiri-haircutting-s1",
      "quote": "It was believed that kamikiri could suddenly appear from nowhere and cut the hair of victims without their noticing.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Korban konon tidak menyadari ketika rambutnya dipotong.",
        "en": "Victims are said not to notice when their hair is cut."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kamikiri-haircutting-c04",
      "source_id": "kamikiri-haircutting-s1",
      "quote": "In Meiji 7 (1874), in Hongō, Tokyo, on the 3rd street at the Suzuki residence, a female servant named Gin was a victim of this kamikiri phenomenon, and this was also reported in the newspaper (Tokyo Nichi Nichi Shimbun) at that time.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Laporan surat kabar tahun 1874 menggambarkan kejadian yang melibatkan pelayan bernama Gin.",
        "en": "An 1874 newspaper report described an incident involving a servant named Gin."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kamikiri-haircutting-c05",
      "source_id": "kamikiri-haircutting-s2",
      "quote": "They go after anyone with hair—men and women, servants and aristocrats.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Satu kisah menggambarkan sasaran dari berbagai jenis kelamin dan lapisan sosial.",
        "en": "One account describes targets across sexes and social ranks."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kamikiri-haircutting-c06",
      "source_id": "kamikiri-haircutting-s2",
      "quote": "They stalk alleys, bathrooms, or other out of the way places.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ia konon mengintai di gang dan kamar mandi.",

      }

      "id": "kamo-taketsunumi-c05",
      "source_id": "kamo-taketsunumi-s1",
      "quote": "Kamotaketsunumi, under the command of Takamimusubi and Amaterasu, descended from the sky to the peak of Tsune in Hyuga and reached Mount Katsuragi in Yamato, where he incarnated as Yatagarasu and led Emperor Jimmu and contributed to his victory.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Satu narasi menyebutnya mengambil wujud Yatagarasu dan membimbing Kaisar Jimmu.",
        "en": "A narrative has him take the form of Yatagarasu and guide Emperor Jimmu."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kamo-taketsunumi-c06",
      "source_id": "kamo-taketsunumi-s1",
      "quote": "Kamotaketsunumi-no-Mikoto had two children, son Tamayorihiko-no-mikoto and daughter Tamayorihime-no-mikoto.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Anak-anaknya bernama Tamayorihiko dan Tamayorihime.",
        "en": "His children are named Tamayorihiko and Tamayorihime."
      }
    }

  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Kanayamahiko adalah dewa dalam mitologi Jepang. Ia dipasangkan dengan dewi Kanayamahime. Pasangan itu ditafsirkan sebagai dewa pertambangan.",
      "en": "Kanayamahiko is a deity in Japanese mythology. He is paired with the goddess Kanayamahime. The pair is interpreted as deities of mines.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "kanayamahiko-c01",
        "kanayamahiko-c02",
        "kanayamahiko-c03"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Kojiki menyebut Kanayamahiko muncul dari muntahan Izanami. Kelahirannya terjadi setelah Izanami terluka akibat melahirkan Kagutsuchi. Versi alternatif Nihon Shoki hanya menyebut Kanayamahiko dalam peristiwa kelahiran ini.",
      "en": "The Kojiki has Kanayamahiko emerge from Izanami’s vomit. His birth follows Izanami’s injuries from bearing Kagutsuchi. A Nihon Shoki variant mentions only Kanayamahiko in this birth episode.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "kanayamahiko-c04",
        "kanayamahiko-c05",
        "kanayamahiko-c06"
      ]
    }
  ],
  "cultural_context": null,
  "etymology": null,
  "story_mode": null,

    {
      "id": "kanayamahiko-s1",

    }
  ],
  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "kanayamahiko-c01",
      "source_id": "kanayamahiko-s1",
      "quote": "金山彦神（かなやまひこのかみ）は、日本神話に登場する神である。天津国玉神の父という説がある。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kanayamahiko adalah dewa dalam mitologi Jepang.",
        "en": "Kanayamahiko is a deity in Japanese mythology."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kanayamahiko-c02",
      "source_id": "kanayamahiko-s1",
      "quote": "『古事記』では金山毘古神、『日本書紀』では金山彦神と表記する。金山毘売神（かなやまびめのかみ、金山姫神）と対偶をなす神である。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ia dipasangkan dengan dewi Kanayamahime.",
        "en": "He is paired with the goddess Kanayamahime."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kanayamahiko-c03",
      "source_id": "kanayamahiko-s1",
      "quote": "「金山」は鉱山と解するのが一般的で、金山毘古神・金山毘売神の二神は鉱山の神と解釈される。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "scholarly-interpretation",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Pasangan itu ditafsirkan sebagai dewa pertambangan.",
        "en": "The pair is interpreted as deities of mines."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kanayamahiko-c04",
      "source_id": "kanayamahiko-s2",
      "quote": "From her vomit (2) was born a deity named Kanayamabiko no kami 金山毗古神. Next [appeared] Kanayamabime no kami 金山毗売神 (3).",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kojiki menyebut Kanayamahiko muncul dari muntahan Izanami.",
        "en": "The Kojiki has Kanayamahiko emerge from Izanami’s vomit."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kanayamahiko-c05",
      "source_id": "kanayamahiko-s1",
      "quote": "神産みにおいて、イザナミが火の神カグツチを産んで火傷をし病み苦しんでいるときに、その嘔吐物（たぐり）から化生した神である。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kelahirannya terjadi setelah Izanami terluka akibat melahirkan Kagutsuchi.",
        "en": "His birth follows Izanami’s injuries from bearing Kagutsuchi."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kanayamahiko-c06",
      "source_id": "kanayamahiko-s1",
      "quote": "『古事記』では金山毘古神・金山毘売神の二神、『日本書紀』の第三の一書では金山彦神のみが化生している。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Versi alternatif Nihon Shoki hanya menyebut Kanayamahiko dalam peristiwa kelahiran ini.",
        "en": "A Nihon Shoki variant mentions only Kanayamahiko in this birth episode."
      }
    }

  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Katawaguruma adalah yōkai Jepang dari cerita gaib lama. Wujudnya kereta beroda tunggal yang terbakar dan membawa perempuan atau laki-laki menakutkan. Satu kisah menyebut ia mengutuk orang yang melihatnya.",
      "en": "Katawaguruma is a Japanese yōkai from old supernatural tales. It takes the form of a flaming single-wheeled cart bearing a woman or frightening man. An account says it curses people who see it.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "katawaguruma-c01",
        "katawaguruma-c02",
        "katawaguruma-c03"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Cerita Shiga menggambarkan penculikan anak perempuan yang mengintipnya. Cerita tersebut berakhir dengan anak dikembalikan setelah ibunya menyesal. Ia konon tidak pernah muncul lagi di desa itu.",
      "en": "A Shiga tale describes the abduction of a curious woman’s child. That tale ends with the child being returned after the mother’s remorse. It is said never to appear in that village again.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "katawaguruma-c04",
        "katawaguruma-c05",
        "katawaguruma-c06"
      ]

  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "katawaguruma-c01",
      "source_id": "katawaguruma-s1",
      "quote": "片輪車（かたわぐるま）は、江戸時代の怪談などの古書に見られる日本の妖怪。炎に包まれた片輪のみの牛車が美女または恐ろしい男を乗せて走り、姿を見たものを祟るとされる。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Katawaguruma adalah yōkai Jepang dari cerita gaib lama.",
        "en": "Katawaguruma is a Japanese yōkai from old supernatural tales."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "katawaguruma-c02",
      "source_id": "katawaguruma-s1",
      "quote": "炎に包まれた片輪のみの牛車が美女または恐ろしい男を乗せて走り、姿を見たものを祟るとされる。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Wujudnya kereta beroda tunggal yang terbakar dan membawa perempuan atau laki-laki menakutkan.",
        "en": "It takes the form of a flaming single-wheeled cart bearing a woman or frightening man."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "katawaguruma-c03",
      "source_id": "katawaguruma-s2",
      "quote": "These demons bestow powerful curses on any who see them.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Satu kisah menyebut ia mengutuk orang yang melihatnya.",
        "en": "An account says it curses people who see it."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "katawaguruma-c04",
      "source_id": "katawaguruma-s2",
      "quote": "In a 17th century record, when a katawaguruma attacked a village in what is now Shiga, she abducted the child of a woman who dared to peek at her through a crack in her door, saying “Instead of watching me you should have been watching your child!”",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Cerita Shiga menggambarkan penculikan anak perempuan yang mengintipnya.",
        "en": "A Shiga tale describes the abduction of a curious woman’s child."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "katawaguruma-c05",
      "source_id": "katawaguruma-s2",
      "quote": "The next night, the katawaguruma came again and saw that the woman was truly regretful. She returned the child unharmed.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Cerita tersebut berakhir dengan anak dikembalikan setelah ibunya menyesal.",
        "en": "That tale ends with the child being returned after the mother’s remorse."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "katawaguruma-c06",
      "source_id": "katawaguruma-s2",
      "quote": "The katawaguruma was never seen in that village again.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ia konon tidak pernah muncul lagi di desa itu.",

  "short_description": {
    "id": "Kawaakago adalah yōkai Jepang yang digambar sebagai bayi di dekat air.",
    "en": "Kawaakago is a Japanese yōkai depicted as a baby near water.",
    "claim_ids": [
      "kawaakago-c01"
    ]
  },
  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Kawaakago adalah yōkai Jepang yang digambar sebagai bayi di dekat air. Ilustrasi Sekien menunjukkan bagian atas tubuhnya di dekat buluh tepi sungai. Kisah belakangan memberinya penampilan bayi kecil berkulit merah.",
      "en": "Kawaakago is a Japanese yōkai depicted as a baby near water. Sekien’s illustration shows its upper body beside riverside reeds. A later account gives it the appearance of a small red-skinned baby.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "kawaakago-c01",
        "kawaakago-c02",
        "kawaakago-c03"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Kisah itu menyebut ia meniru tangisan bayi untuk menarik orang yang lewat. Cerita belakangan menyebut leluconnya dapat berakhir dengan orang tenggelam. Satu tafsir peneliti menganggap gambar itu sebagai permainan kata, bukan makhluk yang tercatat dalam tradisi rakyat.",
      "en": "That account says it imitates a baby’s crying to attract passersby. The later story warns that its prank can end in drowning. One scholarly interpretation regards the image as wordplay rather than a recorded folk creature.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "kawaakago-c04",
        "kawaakago-c05",
        "kawaakago-c06"
      ]
    }
  ],
  "cultural_context": null,
  "etymology": null,
  "story_mode": null,

  "variants": [],
  "stories": [],
  "places": [],
  "timeline": [],
  "relations": [],

    {
      "id": "kawaakago-s2",

  ],
  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "kawaakago-c01",
      "source_id": "kawaakago-s1",
      "quote": "川赤子（かわあかご）は、鳥山石燕の妖怪画集『今昔画図続百鬼』にある日本の妖怪。その名の通り、川、沼、池などの水辺に出現する、赤ん坊の姿の妖怪とされる。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kawaakago adalah yōkai Jepang yang digambar sebagai bayi di dekat air.",
        "en": "Kawaakago is a Japanese yōkai depicted as a baby near water."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kawaakago-c02",
      "source_id": "kawaakago-s1",
      "quote": "『今昔画図続百鬼』によれば、葦の葉の茂った川辺から上半身のみ姿を現した様子が描かれており、川童（=河童）の一種との解説文が添えられている。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ilustrasi Sekien menunjukkan bagian atas tubuhnya di dekat buluh tepi sungai.",
        "en": "Sekien’s illustration shows its upper body beside riverside reeds."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kawaakago-c03",
      "source_id": "kawaakago-s2",
      "quote": "They look like small, red-skinned babies.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "modern-popular-culture",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kisah belakangan memberinya penampilan bayi kecil berkulit merah.",
        "en": "A later account gives it the appearance of a small red-skinned baby."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kawaakago-c04",
      "source_id": "kawaakago-s2",
      "quote": "Kawa akago appear on riverbanks and call out to passersby, perfectly mimicking the sound of crying human babies: “Waah! Waah!” (Japanese: “ Ogyaa! Ogyaa! ”)",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "modern-popular-culture",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kisah itu menyebut ia meniru tangisan bayi untuk menarik orang yang lewat.",
        "en": "That account says it imitates a baby’s crying to attract passersby."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kawaakago-c05",
      "source_id": "kawaakago-s2",
      "quote": "While this is only meant as a prank, some people drown in this manner.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "modern-popular-culture",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Cerita belakangan menyebut leluconnya dapat berakhir dengan orang tenggelam.",
        "en": "The later story warns that its prank can end in drowning."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kawaakago-c06",
      "source_id": "kawaakago-s1",
      "quote": "また妖怪研究家・多田克己は、同画図の川赤子の背景にはいかだと釣竿が描かれており、イトミミズが赤子と呼ばれていたことから、実際に伝承されている妖怪を描いたものではなく、一種の洒落で描かれたものとの説を唱えている。",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "scholarly-interpretation",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Satu tafsir peneliti menganggap gambar itu sebagai permainan kata, bukan makhluk yang tercatat dalam tradisi rakyat.",
        "en": "One scholarly interpretation regards the image as wordplay rather than a recorded folk creature."
      }
    }
  ],
  "conflicts": [],
  "gaps": [
    {
      "field": "images",
      "searched": "Belum ada berkas Wikimedia Commons yang diperiksa untuk entri ini."
    }
  ]
}

  },
  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Kechibi adalah cahaya gaib dalam cerita rakyat Kōchi. Satu kisah menafsirkannya sebagai roh manusia yang berubah menjadi api. Wajah manusia konon mengambang di dalam nyalanya.",
      "en": "Kechibi is a ghost light in Kōchi folklore. One account interprets it as a human spirit turned into fire. A human face is said to float within the flames.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "kechibi-c01",
        "kechibi-c02",
        "kechibi-c03"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Kemunculannya di atas air menghubungkannya dengan hantu kapal dalam beberapa kisah. Kisah lain membedakan roh orang yang tidur dari arwah yang tidak dapat melanjutkan ke alam berikutnya. Sosok itu muncul dalam gulungan bergambar zaman Edo.",
      "en": "Its appearance above water links it with ship ghosts in some accounts. Other accounts distinguish sleeping people’s spirits from souls unable to move on. The figure appears in an Edo-period illustrated scroll.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "kechibi-c04",
        "kechibi-c05",
        "kechibi-c06"
      ]
    }
  ],
  "cultural_context": null,

  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "kechibi-c01",
      "source_id": "kechibi-s1",
      "quote": "Kechibi (けち火) is an atmospheric ghost light told in the legends of Kōchi Prefecture.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kechibi adalah cahaya gaib dalam cerita rakyat Kōchi.",
        "en": "Kechibi is a ghost light in Kōchi folklore."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kechibi-c02",
      "source_id": "kechibi-s1",
      "quote": "It is stated to be the onryō of humans turned into balls of fire, and it is said to be possible to beckon for it by beating a zōri three times, or putting saliva on the zōri and calling for it.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Satu kisah menafsirkannya sebagai roh manusia yang berubah menjadi api.",
        "en": "One account interprets it as a human spirit turned into fire."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kechibi-c03",
      "source_id": "kechibi-s1",
      "quote": "It is also said that the face of a human floats in the fire.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Wajah manusia konon mengambang di dalam nyalanya.",
        "en": "A human face is said to float within the flames."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kechibi-c04",
      "source_id": "kechibi-s1",
      "quote": "They are also said to appear above water, and from this they are sometimes called a type of funayūrei.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kemunculannya di atas air menghubungkannya dengan hantu kapal dalam beberapa kisah.",
        "en": "Its appearance above water links it with ship ghosts in some accounts."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kechibi-c05",
      "source_id": "kechibi-s2",
      "quote": "There are two types of kechibi: those that come out of living humans while they sleep, and those which appear when a soul cannot move on to the next life properly.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kisah lain membedakan roh orang yang tidur dari arwah yang tidak dapat melanjutkan ke alam berikutnya.",
        "en": "Other accounts distinguish sleeping people’s spirits from souls unable to move on."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kechibi-c06",
      "source_id": "kechibi-s2",
      "quote": "They appear in Tōsa obake zōshi , a yōkai picture scroll from the Edo period.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Sosok itu muncul dalam gulungan bergambar zaman Edo.",
        "en": "The figure appears in an Edo-period illustrated scroll."
      }
    }
  ],

  },
  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Keukegen adalah makhluk yang digambar oleh Toriyama Sekien. Ia menyerupai anjing kecil yang tertutup rambut panjang. Namanya juga menjadi permainan kata yang berarti sesuatu yang aneh dan jarang terlihat.",
      "en": "Keukegen is a creature illustrated by Toriyama Sekien. It resembles a small dog covered in long hair. Its name also forms a pun meaning a rarely seen unusual thing.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "keukegen-c01",
        "keukegen-c02",
        "keukegen-c03"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Satu kisah menggambarkannya sebagai roh penyakit di tempat lembap dan gelap. Satu rujukan juga mengaitkannya dengan nasib buruk. Ia digambarkan pemalu dan menghindari kontak dengan manusia.",
      "en": "One account describes it as a disease spirit in damp, dark places. A reference also associates it with bad luck. It is portrayed as shy and avoiding human contact.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "keukegen-c04",
        "keukegen-c05",
        "keukegen-c06"
      ]
    }

      "source_id": "keukegen-s1",
      "quote": "Keukegen (毛羽毛現; \"fluffy hair appearance\") is a creature illustrated in Toriyama Sekien's Konjaku Hyakki Shūi.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Keukegen adalah makhluk yang digambar oleh Toriyama Sekien.",

    {
      "id": "keukegen-c02",
      "source_id": "keukegen-s1",
      "quote": "It resembles a small dog covered entirely in long hair.",
      "locator": "Article text",

      "id": "keukegen-c03",
      "source_id": "keukegen-s1",
      "quote": "Its name is a pun - when written with different kanji (希有怪訝), keukegen means \"an unusual thing which is rarely seen\".",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "etymology",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Namanya juga menjadi permainan kata yang berarti sesuatu yang aneh dan jarang terlihat.",
        "en": "Its name also forms a pun meaning a rarely seen unusual thing."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "keukegen-c04",
      "source_id": "keukegen-s1",
      "quote": "According to one report, the keukegen is a disease spirit which lives in damp, dark places and causes people in the house to get sick.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Satu kisah menggambarkannya sebagai roh penyakit di tempat lembap dan gelap.",
        "en": "One account describes it as a disease spirit in damp, dark places."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "keukegen-c05",
      "source_id": "keukegen-s2",
      "quote": "They are actually a kind of minor spirit of bad luck, disease, and pestilence.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Satu rujukan juga mengaitkannya dengan nasib buruk.",
        "en": "A reference also associates it with bad luck."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "keukegen-c06",
      "source_id": "keukegen-s2",
      "quote": "Being shy by nature, they try to avoid human contact and are rarely seen.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ia digambarkan pemalu dan menghindari kontak dengan manusia.",

  "short_description": {

    "en": "Kidōmaru is an oni in the Kamakura-period Kokon Chomonjū.",
    "claim_ids": [
      "kidomaru-c01"
    ]
  },
  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Kidōmaru adalah oni dalam Kokon Chomonjū dari zaman Kamakura. Satu cerita menyebut Yorimitsu memerintahkan agar ia diikat dengan rantai. Ia memutus rantai dan menyimpan dendam terhadap Yorimitsu.",
      "en": "Kidōmaru is an oni in the Kamakura-period Kokon Chomonjū. A tale has Yorimitsu order him restrained with chains. He breaks the chains and bears a grudge against Yorimitsu.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "kidomaru-c01",
        "kidomaru-c02",
        "kidomaru-c03"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Ia bersembunyi di dalam sapi yang dibunuh sambil menunggu untuk menyergap Yorimitsu. Pertemuan itu berakhir dengan Yorimitsu membunuhnya. Sekien menggambarnya memakai kulit sapi di tengah salju.",
      "en": "He hides inside a slain cow while waiting to ambush Yorimitsu. The encounter ends with Yorimitsu killing him. Sekien depicts him wearing a cow’s hide in the snow.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "kidomaru-c04",
        "kidomaru-c05",
        "kidomaru-c06"
      ]
    }
  ],
  "cultural_context": null,
  "etymology": null,
  "story_mode": null,
  "did_you_know": null,
  "abilities": [],
  "weaknesses": [],
  "variants": [],
  "stories": [],
  "places": [],
  "timeline": [],

  "tradition_vs_modern": null,

      "title": "Kidōmaru",
      "publisher": "Wikipedia",
      "language": "en",
      "type": "wikipedia",
      "author": null
    }
  ],
  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "kidomaru-c01",
      "source_id": "kidomaru-s1",
      "quote": "Kidōmaru (鬼童丸, 鬼同丸) is an oni that appears in the Kamakura period collection of setsuwa, the Kokon Chomonjū, among other sources.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kidōmaru adalah oni dalam Kokon Chomonjū dari zaman Kamakura.",
        "en": "Kidōmaru is an oni in the Kamakura-period Kokon Chomonjū."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kidomaru-c02",
      "source_id": "kidomaru-s1",
      "quote": "Yorimitsu said that Yorinobu was careless, so they should restrain the oni with chains, and stayed at Yorinobu's house for that night.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Satu cerita menyebut Yorimitsu memerintahkan agar ia diikat dengan rantai.",
        "en": "A tale has Yorimitsu order him restrained with chains."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kidomaru-c03",
      "source_id": "kidomaru-s1",
      "quote": "Kidōmaru easily tore off those chains, and with a grudge against Yorimitsu, looked at his bed and kept watch.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ia memutus rantai dan menyimpan dendam terhadap Yorimitsu.",
        "en": "He breaks the chains and bears a grudge against Yorimitsu."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kidomaru-c04",
      "source_id": "kidomaru-s1",
      "quote": "Yorimitsu noticed this and told a servant, \"tomorrow, I will make a visit to the Kurama temple.\" Kidōmaru then went ahead to Kurama, killed one free-ranging cattle at the Ichihara field, hid inside its body, and waited for Yorimitsu to come.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ia bersembunyi di dalam sapi yang dibunuh sambil menunggu untuk menyergap Yorimitsu.",
        "en": "He hides inside a slain cow while waiting to ambush Yorimitsu."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kidomaru-c05",
      "source_id": "kidomaru-s1",
      "quote": "It is said that Kidōmaru appeared from inside the cow and tried to slash at Yorimitsu, but Yorimitsu struck down Kidōmaru with a single strike.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Pertemuan itu berakhir dengan Yorimitsu membunuhnya.",
        "en": "The encounter ends with Yorimitsu killing him."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kidomaru-c06",
      "source_id": "kidomaru-s1",
      "quote": "In the yōkai pictures collection, the Konjaku Hyakki Shūi by Toriyama Sekien, under the title of \"Kidō,\" it depicts Kidōmaru in the snow wearing the skin of a cattle waiting for Yorimitsu to come.",
      "locator": "Article text",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Sekien menggambarnya memakai kulit sapi di tengah salju.",
        "en": "Sekien depicts him wearing a cow’s hide in the snow."
      }
    }
  ],

      "en": "Kinoko are yōkai in legends of the Kinki region. Some traditions place them in mountains and relate them to yamawaro. They resemble young children dressed in leaves or blue clothing.",
